# [3    The Ufungamano Initiative](#toc.xhtml_ch-03)

## [A movement of movements](#toc.xhtml_ch-03)

## Introduction

On December 15, 1999, two simultaneous and intimately related events dramatically heightened contentions in the Kenyan constitutional reform process. The first was a gathering of over 50 organisations drawn mainly from religious groups, secular civil society, and opposition political parties at Ufungamano House in Nairobi, to launch what they framed as a people-driven constitutional reform process. The second was the establishment of a Parliamentary Select Committee (PSC) by KANU and its partner, the National Development Party (NDP), to spearhead a parliament-led review process. These two processes were manifestly opposed to each other’s existence.The Ufungamano House meeting was convened by the Religious Community Coordination Team (RCCT), which included representatives from the Catholic Church’s Kenya Episcopal Conference (KEC), the Supreme Council of Kenyan Muslims (SUPKEM), the National Council of Churches of Kenya (NCCK), the Hindu Council of Kenya, the Muslim Consultative Council (MCC), the Organisation of African Instituted Churches, the Anglican Church of Kenya (ACK), the Presbyterian Church of Eastern Africa (PCEA), and the Methodist Church in Kenya (MCK). Joining religious groups in this gathering were secular civil society organisations that had previously congregated within the NCEC. The meeting aimed to find a consensual way forward out of an impasse created by the Moi regime’s continued refusal to meet popular demands for a people-driven constitutional review process. Stakeholders identified in the 1998 Constitution of Kenya Review Commission Amendment Act – including KANU and opposition political parties – were invited. However, KANU, the Shirikisho Party, the Kenya Social Congress (KSC), and a section of the National Development Party (NDP) – specifically its leader, Raila Odinga – all boycotted the event.A significant outcome of the two-day consultation was the establishment of the People’s Commission of Kenya (PCK), which was tasked with leading Kenyans in a comprehensive review of the constitution by collecting and collating their views under the banner of what came to be known as the Ufungamano Initiative. A total of 54 NGOs, social movements, political parties, and political movements united under this banner to lobby for a new constitutional order in Kenya (Ogony 2004; Andreassen and Tostensen 2006; Kindiki 2007). Arguably, no other constitutional reform movement has challenged the state so effectively in Kenya’s recent history.As consultations unfolded at Ufungamano House, KANU and its NDP partners took advantage of the absence of most opposition legislators from parliament to set up the rival PSC under the chairmanship of NDP leader Raila Odinga (Karanja 2008). The PSC consisted of 14 KANU and 13 opposition legislators, with the latter mostly drawn from Odinga’s NDP as most of the other opposition parties, including the Democratic Party (DP), Ford Kenya, and the Social Democratic Party (SDP), refused to take part, opting instead to align themselves with the Ufungamano Initiative.These developments were significant triggers of a new wave of intensive contention that pitted the Ufungamano Initiative – comprising the majority of civil society (both secular and religious) and opposition political parties – against the PSC, which enjoyed support among a smaller number of religious organisations, such as the Evangelical Fellowship of Kenya and its member churches (i.e. the Africa Inland Church, the African Independent Pentecostal Church of Africa, the Deliverance Church, the Redeemed Gospel Church of Kenya, and the Church of God in East Africa). Many other stakeholders were caught somewhere between the two opposing poles.The emergence of two parallel review processes highlights the rapid deterioration in relations between political parties and religious institutions in Kenya, given that they had conspired to draft the IPPG deal at the peak of the NCEC campaigns in 1997\. The Ufungamano Initiative therefore marked a significant realignment of forces as activist religious organisations joined hands with secular civil society groups and the political opposition to challenge the state. This leads to three key questions. What led different social and political forces to unite under the Ufungamano Initiative? What “political” opportunities allowed the Ufungamano Initiative to emerge? What new agenda and strategies did the Ufungamano Initiative bring to the Kenyan constitutional reform struggle?Like its predecessors, the Ufungamano Initiative epitomised intra- and inter-ethnic, religious, generational, and class power politics. Furthermore, its broad-based character imbued it with multiple contradictions. This chapter identifies the main players behind the formation of the Ufungamano Initiative and analyses their relationships and the glue that held them together. It argues that it was framing by Ufungamano Initiative entrepreneurs that mobilised a collective identity among a large number of disparate groups. This framing – which also appealed to the wider public – centred on shared political grievances.The chapter uses a socio-historical approach that traces the emergence of the Ufungamano Initiative to the various actors and issues in the constitutional reform process in Kenya. This approach helps to explain the initiative’s emergence, its structure and certain aspects of its operations, and its achievements and limitations. The chapter first focuses on the types of players involved in the emergence of the initiative. The aim here is to bring to the surface the generative contradictions during the formative stages of the Ufungamano Initiative. This is followed by an explanation of how and why the Ufungamano Initiative emerged. Specifically, this is an attempt to theorise the movement’s emergence. Here, the prevailing political environment is explored to show why the leadership of movement “entrepreneurs” catalysed discontent towards the formation of a new movement.

## The typology of actors in the Ufungamano Initiative-led wave of contention

According to Tilly (2004: 12), social movements are:Not solo performances, [but] interactions between temporarily connected (and often shifting) groups of claimants and the objects of their claims [whereby] third parties such as constituents, allies, rival claimants, enemies, authorities, and various publics often play significant parts in the campaigns.As such, he continues, “we will never explain social movements’ variations and claimants without paying close attention to political actors, other than the central claimants” (Tilly 2004: 12). Therefore, to appreciate the idiosyncrasies and intricacies of Kenyan struggles, it is necessary to look at the dynamic composition of – and relationships between – the claimants and their targets. These relationships were mostly dictated by congruencies and/or divergences of means and goals between actors.Taking into account actors’ organisational identities, their various stances on the process of constitutional reform, and their relationships with similar and differently oriented organisations and the state, the key players in the Ufungamano Initiative-led wave of contention may be divided into three main groups: those who advocated people-centred reform; those who were opposed to people-centred reform; and those who were neutral or indifferent to such reform. Each of these categories included a variety of socioeconomic groups, but each was dominated by a particular elite faction. This points to the fact that elite fragmentation provided the political opportunity for the movement’s emergence and operations. Below, I discuss the three categories in greater detail.

### People-centred constitutional reform actors

This was the group that coalesced around the Ufungamano Initiative. It brought together at least three types of actors. The first were combative, activist, human rights, civil society organisations that had previously congregated under the NCEC banner (see [Chapter 2](#chapter02.xhtml)). Among their number were subalterns, students, youth and women’s movements, all of whom faced everyday struggles and campaigned on a range of socioeconomic issues. Secondly, there were cautious members of the middle class, primarily represented by religious organisations. According to Mutua (2008: 124), their position in popular struggles has continually shifted between support for radical groups and acceptance of the status quo, “depending on personal, institutional, and external factors.” Finally, the Ufungamano Initiative included a number of opposition political parties whose “government-in-waiting syndrome rendered them generally unprincipled and subject of corruption by the ruling bloc; its interest in reforms not centred on democratization of the state, but on minimum changes to make its ascendancy to power possible” (Mutua 2008: 123).It is important to understand how these disparate groups managed to set aside their differences in the aftermath of the IPPG deal. The explanation lies in the closed system practised by the Moi/KANU regime. As North *et al*.’s (2013) Limited Access Orders model explains, exclusion tends to cultivate conditions for a coalition of the excluded. These are likely to gravitate towards protest movements or even violence as a way of collectively challenging their condition. M. Odhiambo offers a similar explanation for why this rather large so-called pro-reform movement evolved, and why its inherent cleavages became emblematic of this particular struggle:From the 1990s, we had a coming together of what is loosely called a pro-reform movement and pro-reform politicians, largely responding to the closed Moi/KANU system, into a movement that took constitutional reform as its key focus. But there have been different points of emphasis. Opposition politicians always emphasised aspects that would bring them closer to power. On the other hand, the common man [*sic*], in a context where we have very high levels of poverty, has always thought that the struggle is about basic rights, access to improved living conditions, and generally issues of bread and butter.¹These cleavages are important for explaining the fragility of alliances formed in the pro-reform camp. Indeed, they raise a couple of critical questions about this struggle. Specifically, where did the different positions in the camp converge? And did the dominant elite voices in the camp – who were preoccupied with the Nkrumaist cliché “seek ye the political power and all the rest will flow” – understand the subaltern struggle and at least try to move it into the mainstream?² There is evidence that subaltern voices usually fell on deaf ears when directed at those seeking political power. On the other hand, the elite could also fail to agree among themselves, hence the fragmentations that sometimes forced strategic alignments with subalterns in order to challenge the dominant elite force. The emergence of the NCEC and the Ufungamano Initiative are cases in point.However, such coalescence was generally characterised by co-optive relationships, and betrayals of the subalterns were commonplace. This explains why the most frequent outcome of improvised convergence for subalterns was that nothing changed for them. Even when there was some change, the pace was painfully slow and always measured, primarily due to internal opposition.

### State-centred constitutional reform actors

The second major category of actors comprised those who opposed people-centred constitutional reform and coalesced under the PSC. The key players here were the Moi/KANU regime itself and its chief collaborators – the NDP, the Shirikisho Party, the KSC, and pro-state civil society organisations represented by the Evangelical Fellowship of Kenya and its affiliated churches (Karanja 2008; Mutua 2008). According to Mutua (2008), the actors in this group, which represented a faction of the elite that has historically held power in Kenya, pushed for the undemocratic centralisation of power in the presidency and a small cadre of supporters. Hence, Mutua (2008: 123) labels them “opaque, despotic and resistant to democratic transformation.” They have been – and remain – the dominant political and economic force in Kenya’s intra-elite struggle because, over the years, they have managed to subordinate society to their whims while also frustrating attempts at reform through a combination of coercion, co-option, manipulation, and violence (Katumanga 1999). Actors within this camp were the direct targets of the Ufungamano Initiative.

### Neutral/indifferent actors

The third group comprised actors who seemed either neutral or indifferent to the process of reform. According to Mutua (2008: 124), they were ideologically “pro-establishment,” given their “distrust and distance from the more vocal factions of civil society or even the political opposition.” He suggests that they, like most of Kenya’s elite, were drawn from “the middle” and had “vast interests in land, property, the professions and the marketplace. The middle is ethnically, racially, regionally, and religiously diverse, a fact that makes it formidable” (Mutua 2008: 124). Because of its size, a tipping point is inevitable if this group chooses to affiliate with a particular side in Kenya’s incessant intra-elite struggles.

### Divisions within the three groups

Notwithstanding these three broad categories, there was no uniformity in any of the camps. Rather, there were spectrums among the supporters of a people-led process, those who favoured a government-led process, and those who were neutral on the issue. All three camps included groups and individuals drawn from a variety of political parties, civil society, and the private sector.When the advocates of a people-led process gathered at Ufungamano House on December 15–16, 1999 and announced the formation of the PCK to collect and collate the views of all Kenyans, the Ufungamano Initiative succeeded where its predecessors, including the NCEC, had failed.³ Specifically, it brought together a broad spectrum of pro-reform activists, ordinary people, and influential citizens’ groups, including political parties, religious institutions, and human rights organisations, to mount a coordinated and sustained challenge to the Moi/KANU political edifice through direct action and the demand for a new constitution.How did such diverse groups manage to converge under a single umbrella in a political context that was still rife with suspicion due to a long litany of betrayal? What had changed with regard to political opportunity and constraint? The following section will attempt to answer these questions.

## The emergence of the Ufungamano Initiative: the why and the how

Three closely interwoven, mutually reinforcing explanatory variables contributed to the emergence of the Ufungamano Initiative. The first was the denial of claimants’ rights. The second was the agency offered by religious leaders who mobilised discontent among various previously organised but disparate actors. And the third was the political context, including multifarious opportunities and constraints. None of these three variables is sufficient, in itself, to explain the emergence of the Ufungamano Initiative. Rather, they all buttressed one another. The prevailing political conditions were important as they generated discontent, which in turn motivated participation in activism. And there was a political opportunity to transform that discontent into a movement, but it needed the agency of Kenya’s religious leaders to make it happen. Below, I explain all of these variables and their interplay in the emergence of the Ufungamano Initiative.

### Relative deprivation

For many years, the nexus of relative deprivation, denied rights, and injustice has been advanced as an explanation for the emergence of collective action. The likes of Osaghae (2008), Buechler (2000), Gurr (1970), Davies (1962), and Geschwender (1968) have all argued that mass protest occurs when people subjectively perceive themselves to be lacking resources that are enjoyed by a particular reference group. Eventually, the substantial gap between what they have come to expect and what they actually possess becomes intolerable and they join forces to alter the situation (Davies 1962; Buechler 2000).Following these authors, I will show that discontent over the Moi/KANU state’s broken promises over wider participation in the constitution-drafting process created the sense of relative deprivation that fuelled the Ufungamano Initiative. Such reversals, as Davies (1962: 5–6) notes, “force” people to “subjectively fear that ground gained with great effort will be quite lost; their mood becomes revolutionary.”

### Leadership and structural context

The leadership and structural context variable borrows from the resource mobilisation model’s emphasis on resources as key for movements’ emergence, development, success, and failure. Specifically, as was noted in [Chapter 2](#chapter02.xhtml), social movements develop only when individuals with grievances are able to mobilise sufficient resources to take action (McCarthy and Zald 1977). As shall become clear later in this chapter, Kenya’s religious leaders were a critical resource that enabled a host of actors aggrieved by KANU’s broken promises to unite under the Ufungamano Initiative. They did this by framing the current context as a political opportunity to mobilise for collective action. As such, framing was useful in the social construction of grievances and channelling the same into a movement as a corrective to ameliorate unacceptable conditions.

### Political opportunities and constraints

The Ufungamano Initiative emerged in a context of expanding political opportunities. These opportunities resulted from earlier cycles of contention (discussed [Chapter 2](#chapter02.xhtml)) that had managed to expand spaces for contestation. The IPPG package, the 1997 Constitution of Kenya Review Act, the outcome of the 1997 general election, and the subsequent negotiations that came to be known as the Safari Park Process (on which the Moi/KANU regime later reneged) were the political opportunities that incubated the Ufungamano Initiative. These opportunities are explained in detail below.

### The IPPG deal, the general election, and its aftermath

As part of the IPPG deal of October 1997, the Moi/KANU regime agreed to some minimal administrative reforms to electoral laws, but it dissolved parliament before these were anchored in the constitution. Nonetheless, the reforms significantly expanded the political opportunity structures for opposition groups. First, there was a restructuring of the Electoral Commission to include ten new members nominated by opposition political parties.⁴ Second, there was an agreement that 12 specially nominated members of parliament would be allocated to political parties on the basis of their strength in the legislature after the 1997 election. Third, there was the abrogation of the Sedition Act, to enable freedom of expression. Fourth, limits were imposed on state power through amendments to the notorious Chiefs’ Authority Act and the Preservation of Public Security Act. Finally, and most importantly, the Constitution of Kenya Review Act of 1997 (hereafter 1997 Review Act) was enacted. Most opposition political parties saw these as positive developments. On the other hand, a few – including a section of Ford Asili and, interestingly, the NDP – sided with the NCEC in dismissing them as inadequate, and suggested that, without constitutional anchorage, President Moi would continue to manipulate the political scene.⁵Notwithstanding these misgivings, the IPPG did offer new political opportunities that were crucial in the post-1997 general election mobilisations. For instance, one of the most ardent critics of the IPPG, former NCEC co-convener Kivutha Kibwana, argued:IPPG emerged as a response to a politically charged environment that had radicalised demands by the NCEC. The state responded by trying to address these demands through the IPPG, which, even though rejected by the NCEC, weakened Moi’s power. Things we were not aware of before started happening: his way of running everything, guarding turf, etc., was weakened.⁶Another prominent figure in the pro-reform movement and one-time leader of the 4Cs, Paddy Onyango, similarly captured the impact of the IPPG deal on the constitutional change struggle:The IPPG gave us a window of opportunity. For example, the Preservation of Public Security Act was reviewed and therefore the right to assemble was made easier … Secondly, the knowledge that you could demonstrate without being taken to Nyayo House⁷ was in itself liberating and gave us a good opportunity to mobilise.⁸However, the IPPG deal also “punctured the reform momentum in the sense that, with the 1997 election approaching, the actual interest of the opposition was exposed as they crossed over to make deals with KANU.”⁹ There are conflicting accounts for why this happened. One is the weakness of the leadership of the civil society-led wave. Specifically, reversals of the gains by the NCEC-led contention resulted from the leaders’ failure to acknowledge early enough that the interests of their political colleagues were not necessarily consonant with the aspirations and spirit of civil society.^(10) The second view is that the political elites were uncomfortable with the civil society-led mass actions of 1997 and needed an excuse to clip civil society’s growing power and influence. They designed and/or embraced the IPPG principally to do exactly that.^(11)Nonetheless, others read the IPPG as an expression of some goodwill from the ruling party. As already mentioned, one of the significant outcomes of the IPPG deal, albeit dismissed by some as a politicians’ red herring to steal the thunder of civil society on the issue of constitutional reform, was the 1997 Review Act. This created a reference point that became significant in the post-1997 election mobilisations because it was considered sufficient for a number of reasons. For a start, it spelt out how the Constitution of Kenya Review Commission (hereafter the Commission) was to be constituted to implement the review. Second, it acknowledged the need to involve all Kenyans in the process. This had been a key demand of the NCEC-allied groups. In responding to this demand, the 1997 Review Act endeared itself to a good number of contenders who, for once, truly believed that they would be able to participate. Specifically, its First Schedule listed the following the stakeholders with a legitimate claim for representation in the review rrocess: political parties; religious organisations; women’s organisations; civil society; and the attorney general.However, the 1997 Review Act fell short of spelling out how the review process would actually take place. Its main undoing was its exclusivity in its definition of stakeholders. This was compounded by a lack of clarity on the formula for constituting the Commission. All the same, the IPPG deal and the resultant 1997 Review Act broadened access to institutional participation in the constitution-making process. Moreover, they confirmed that, with greater pressure, the Moi/KANU regime was vulnerable and open to negotiation and making concessions. In the end, despite its flaws, the 1997 Review Act offered an important rallying point in the accountability politics and protests against the regime after Moi/KANU regime reneged on the promises it had made, and led to the emergence of the Ufungamano Initiative.Kenyans went to the polls at the end of 1997, and Moi/KANU yet again defeated a divided opposition that had ignored its supporters’ pleas to unite. The *Weekly Review* (1998a: 5) observed that Moi’s win – albeit with a reduced majority of 108 MPs against the opposition’s 102 – was not unexpected, as Kenyans had voted along ethnic lines:Each of the major presidential candidates from the majority ethnic communities … [had] count[ed] on their tribal arithmetic to upset KANU. This was not to be. As Luoland voted overwhelmingly for Raila Odinga, Kalenjinland for Moi, Luhyaland for Kijana Wamalwa, and Kikuyuland for Kibaki, many of the smaller of the 42 Kenyan tribes sided with Moi.In the month of the election (December 1997) as well as the next three months, politically instigated and state-sponsored ethnic violence broke out in various multi-tribal districts, especially the Rift Valley and Coast provinces, just as had happened in the 1992 general election. This violence was directed specifically at ethnic groups who were suspected of being predominantly pro-opposition, and the leaderships of various opposition parties, civil society, and religious groups united to condemn them (*Weekly Review* 1998a; 1998b; 1998c; 1998d; 1998e; 1998f). However, divisions within the political opposition elite continued to play out when Raila Odinga and Kijana Wamalwa accepted the presidential election result and congratulated Moi as the winner, while Mwai Kibaki, the runner-up, rejected the results and called for a rerun (*Weekly Review* 1998a).The New Year began on a low note. The speed with which opposition political parties and religious leaders who had pushed for reform fell for the Moi/KANU bait in the form of the IPPG deal left plenty of the radical elements in the NCEC with egg on their faces (Mutua 2008: 109; Katumanga 1999). Indeed, some politicians and journalists, wary of the growing influence of the NCEC, were already calling for its dissolution, arguing that it had exhausted its mandate (Kibwana 2000). The NCEC’s saving grace, paradoxically, came almost immediately after the election, courtesy of Moi’s behaviour towards the opposition and the constitutional reforms. He reneged on every promise he had made to other politicians in the IPPG deal.Moi/KANU’s post-election strategy was multifaceted. With a reduced majority in parliament, the president reached out to one of his erstwhile nemeses, Raila Odinga, who now led the third-largest party in the legislature. This olive branch ultimately led to a merger of KANU and the NDP on March 18, 2002 under the name KANU, with Moi as party president and Odinga as secretary-general. Even before the merger, the Moi/KANU regime, with the support of Odinga’s NDP, had started the systematic mutilation of the 1997 Review Act and, in effect, some of the key IPPG agreements.The Moi–Odinga partnership and their subsequent manipulation of the review process became the principal reference point upon which anger and bitterness over denied rights to participate in the review process emerged. Many who had invested their faith and hope in the promises of the IPPG felt betrayed. As a result, a broad spectrum of seemingly strange bedfellows – opposition politicians, religious leaders, and more radical, NCEC-allied civil society groups – became allies to seek collective action solutions to the deteriorating political situation.

### Post-1997 general election cooperation and conflict

To appreciate the intricacies of the struggles that ensued after the election, one needs to look at the fluid relations between and within the various claimants and their targets. These relationships go a long way to explaining the emergence of religious leaders as a mediating force between competing interests and positions. Over time, their mediating role, combined with the Moi/KANU regime’s contempt for other constitutional review contenders and widespread socioeconomic and political dysfunction, gave rise to the Ufungamano Initiative. The core of this contention was the operationalisation of the 1997 Review Act.In the aftermath of general election, the NCEC continued to argue that the IPPG deal was fundamentally flawed (Kibwana 2000; Mutua 2008). Furthermore, it cast doubt on the Moi/KANU regime’s commitment to shepherding a pro-people process (Kibwana 2000). The NCEC was ultimately proved correct as stakeholders sought to redress the flaws in the 1997 Review Act through a series of consultations aimed at agreeing on norms, institutions, and processes for constitutional reform. These talks – dubbed the Bomas, Safari Park I, Safari Park II, and Safari Park III processes^(12) – revealed deep cleavages that derailed, albeit temporarily, the reform train. More fundamentally, these cleavages provided the Moi/KANU regime with an opportunity to try to wrestle control of the reform process from civil society.The political opposition parties seemed torn between supporting civil society “positions” that they did not fully embrace or continuing in the IPPG spirit of cooperation with KANU (Kibwana 2000; Mutua 2008). In mid-1998, they chose to support the latter by joining the regime to form the Inter-Parties Parliamentary Committee, which invited other key stakeholders identified in the 1997 Review Act to the Bomas and Safari Park talks. The political opposition’s argument was that a parliamentary review process was legally superior to the extra-parliamentary one that NCEC-led civil society was advocating. Moreover, it felt that locating the process in parliament meant they were firmly in control and had relegated civil society to a peripheral role.^(13)However, reality gradually dawned on the opposition parties as time progressed. By mid-1999, they were forced to turn to the very civil society groups they had previously shunned. This happened because, in their naivety, the political parties had allowed the attorney general (a presidential appointee) to convene the stakeholder consultations that were meant to address the defects in the 1997 Review Act. But the NCEC-allied actors remained opposed to a parliamentary-centred process. They rightly felt – as was later confirmed by *Njoya and 6 Others vs. Attorney General & 3 Others* – that parliament could only *alter* the constitution. Section 27 specified that it “had no jurisdiction or power … to *abrogate* the existing constitution and enact a new one in its place” (Kenya Law Reports 2004: 262; emphasis added). The principal argument was that the power to draft a new constitution rested with the people of Kenya as a whole. As such, the NCEC-allied groups argued, because parliament was itself a creation of the constitution, it was improper for it to review itself or define what its role in the review process should be. Only the Kenyan people could review the constitution and define the roles of the various actors in such a process (Kenya Law Reports 2004). Hence, ordinary Kenyans’ involvement in a radical review of the constitution was the NCEC-allied groups’ key demand during the Safari Park and Bomas negotiations.KANU sought to exclude the NCEC and its allies from the Safari Park talks because of their insistence on an extra-parliamentary national convention to write the country’s new constitution (Mutua 2008). However, the regime was prepared to grant “neutral or indifferent” groups (see above) some places around the negotiating table. It also proposed – against the grain of the 1997 Review Act – that all appointments to the Commission should be along district lines (*Weekly Review* 1998g; 1998h). But opposition political parties, the NCEC, the LSK, and religious organisations all vigorously opposed this, identifying it as an attempt to ethnicise the review process (*Weekly Review* 1998g; 1998h). KANU finally relented under the pressure and what was then seen as a genuine consensus for cooperation among different stakeholders was reached during the Safari Park negotiations (*Weekly Review* 1998g).The Safari Park agreement of October 1998 contained several vital features and a number of amendments to the 1997 Review Act that later defined the nature of the grievances that informed the emergence and operations of the Ufungamano Initiative when the Moi/Odinga faction reneged on its promises. The agreement constituted what some stakeholders considered to be a contract between the different stakeholders, parliament, and the government of Kenya to review the constitution legitimately and within the law. The Constitution of Kenya Review Commission Amendment Act of 1998 that emerged out of these negotiations was viewed as a sign of goodwill among the political elites to take the review process forward.^(14) Because this act was negotiated outside parliament by a majority of stakeholders who were not parliamentary political parties, its enactment suggested that the political elites agreed that the whole process should be divorced from executive and parliamentary control as much as possible.However, the consensus proved short-lived. The KANU/NDP alliance deliberately ramped up the confusion at the end of the final drafting process by including a new clause that made the act’s implementation problematic (Kibwana 2000), specifically in the schedule that named the so-called “stakeholders.” This list was exclusionary, as only a few stakeholders were explicitly identified. The act therefore locked out many groups that had equal or even superior claims of legitimacy^(15) such as grassroots groups representing the urban poor, including Muungano wa Wanavijiji, the National Youth Movement, and the National Youth Lobby for Reforms.^(16) These and other claimants insisted that their exclusion seriously undermined the credibility and legitimacy of the process, which they interpreted as an elite pact that recognised only a few elite formations as bona fide stakeholders with legitimate claims to representation in the Commission.^(17) Moreover, as Mutua (2008: 111) notes, “the bane of the Safari Park compromise was the composition of the CKRC [Constitution of Kenya Review Commission] … [T]he formula for doling out the slots … was not written into law.”In the event, in February 1999, the process of appointing the commissioners reached a stalemate as KANU and the opposition failed to reach agreement on how to share out the 13 slots allocated to political parties. Indeed, “KANU walked out of a meeting called by religious leaders to mediate an agreement between the political parties.”^(18) The desire of various groups – particularly political parties – to secure their interests by nominating only commissioners from their own ranks lent further credence to the grievances of those who had been omitted from the list of stakeholders.^(19) In turn, this generated further antagonism towards the political elite. A youth participant in the Ufungamano Initiative recalled:In this process we thought we were kindred spirits … but some took off to the other side when they were assured of something. Take the Honourable Phoebe Asiyo, for example. At Safari Park, we were together – youth and women negotiating together. In Ufungamano we were together, agitating and piling pressure on KANU and the NDP… But when the Kenya Women’s Political Caucus was assured of slots, I remember one morning Phoebe Asiyo was with us at the Ufungamano, and in the afternoon she was being interviewed by Raila Odinga to be a commissioner in the parliamentary-led process … [T]here were people furthering only their self-interests. The political elite’s mistake, however, was to think they could take the youth for granted.^(20)Under the circumstances, it was unsurprising that a group of entrepreneurs utilised the exclusionary nature of the Constitution of Kenya Review Commission Amendment Act as an opportunity to frame contention and the entire enterprise of the resultant Ufungamano Initiative.

### The Kenya Women’s Political Caucus’s role in incubating the Ufungamano Initiative

The important role played by women leaders in incubating the Ufungamano Initiative is often overlooked. Immediately after the enactment of the 1998 Constitution of Kenya Review Commission Amendment Act, the squabbling among political parties over the distribution of commissioners’ slots prompted two largely unsung women leaders – Phoebe Asiyo and Julia Ojiambo^(21) – to approach religious leaders and ask them to mediate.^(22) Moreover, they urged these religious leaders to seize the opportunity to unite the fragmented opposition. This led to the birth of the Religious Community Coordination Team (RCCT) – the immediate predecessor to the Ufungamano Initiative.^(23) A confidential RCCT document dated September 28, 1999 and titled *Strategy for the People-based Constitution of Kenya Review Process by the Religious Community in the Republic of Kenya*, the minutes of various meetings, and other internal documents provide incontrovertible evidence of the crucial role played by the Kenya Women’s Political Caucus in these developments.^(24)The initial activities of the RCCT were crafted as a mediation intervention to break the deadlock among the political parties. The first meeting took place in April 1999 and was chaired by the head of the Anglican Church in Kenya – Archbishop David Gitari. All of the parliamentary political parties, including KANU, attended and the meeting made what appeared to be substantial progress. KANU’s representatives – the party’s secretary-general and cabinet minister Julius Sunkuli – gave up one of its seats on the Commission, reducing its allocation from seven to six. The NDP and Ford Kenya then followed suit, each relinquishing their single slot.^(25) However, while it appeared that the RCCT was negotiating a way through the stalemate, Moi dismissed the agreements and refused to meet the religious leaders, despite their pleas.^(26)

### Shifting gears and heightened contention

In May 1999, amid the ongoing party political acrimony, Moi, buoyed by his newfound strength that KANU’s cooperation with Odinga’s NDP had produced, announced a major assault on both the Review Act and 1998 Safari Park stakeholder agreements. He relaunched an old debate when authoritatively stating that parliament should be the only body to review the constitution. Rather than spending the 4.5 billion shillings already allocated on the review, he stated that this money would be used for poverty-eradication programmes. He further declared that a popular, people-centred process was unnecessary; instead, he would invite two experienced foreign lawyers to write the constitution and present it to parliament for debate and ratification.^(27)In Moi’s reasoning, “Wanjiku” (denoting an ordinary Kenyan female) was neither interested nor qualified to participate in a comprehensive reform preocess. For him, busybodies in the NGOs had been masquerading as “the people” and demanding a new constitution; yet, nobody had elected them. Of significance here is the fact that this was not the first time Moi reneged on a commitment to deliver a new constitution. As such, it was not lost on the RCCT that the very institution that Moi and his sympathisers claimed should oversee the review process – parliament – had created the stalemate. Moreover, other stakeholders – especially those allied to the NCEC – were already opposed to parliament as it was heavily controlled by the executive, which would only sanction a process it could dominate.^(28)Secular civil society (mainly NGOs and grassroots movements), religious organisations, and what remained of the opposition political parties were alarmed by Moi’s announcement and shifted gear as it became apparent that the regime would not relent. Those opposed to Moi and KANU realised that if anything were to change, unity and a new strategy were paramount. These groups identified Moi’s chicanery as the single greatest threat to the peview process (Mutua 2008). They further wondered why foreigners should be involved in a process that was a purely Kenyan affair. Moreover, why was Moi attempting to derail a process that had been agreed in open negotiations, enacted by parliament, and assented by himself? In its opposition, the RCCT framed Moi’s proposed formula as illegal, and declared that they “could not, in conscience, sit by and continue to watch the flagrant disregard of the law … [they therefore] moved in to fulfil the law, not to break it” (RCCT 1999). They framed their objection to Moi’s machinations as based on “justice and fairness founded on *morality and the will of the people*,” which in their view was “the highest law” (ibid.).The RCCT, together with Kenya Women’s Political Caucus, now started reaching out to civil society and like-minded opposition political parties to build a consensus on how to salvage the process and allow for popular participation in the reforms.^(29) In June 1999, the RCCT met with the Kenya Women’s Political Caucus to plan a way forward and guide Kenya out of what they framed as a “deep crisis [epitomized by] the slime of corruption, environmental degradation, robberies, murders, family violence, ethnic violence, general insecurity, land grabbing and many other vices” (RCCT 1999: 4). They posited a comprehensive review of the basic rules of governance as the only antidote to this malaise. Here, we see the framing moving from diagnostic and prognostic to motivational as during the meeting it was suggested that the religious community should assume control of the review process and steer it in line with the spirit of the 1997 act, as amended in 1998\. The proposal was that stakeholders from secular civil society and opposition political parties should unite under the stewardship of religious leaders, now seen as neutral arbiters,^(30) to conduct an extra-parliamentary review process (RCCT 1999). The claims and rights of the Kenyan people to make their own constitution were explicitly articulated during this meeting. Specifically, the framing was that “a country’s constitution is a covenant which all people of a nation commit to govern themselves in justice, peace and prosperity for all … and all the people of Kenya should be fully involved” (RCCT 1999: 5).At the same meeting, a lean taskforce comprising four people – Dr. Julia Ojiambo (Kenya Women’s Political Caucus, Said Athman (SUPKEM), James Mageria, and the Rev. Harold Mwang’ombe (ACK) – was established to develop a strategy to make a “people-based constitutional Review Process led by the religious community possible” (RCCT 1999). Three months later, on September 3, 1999, the taskforce presented its *Strategy for the People-based Constitution of Kenya Review Process by the Religious Community in the Republic of Kenya* (ibid.) to the RCCT. This document outlined a clear plan for how to establish such a process, complete with timelines. A key recommendation was for the RCCT to convene on October 13–14, 1999 to review and approve the strategy. Over the previous three months (since the June 1999 meeting), the RCCT and the Kenya Women’s Political Caucus had reached out to all of the opposition political parties, the NCEC, and other civil society organisations to stress the need to come together. From these consultations, these groups reached a consensus on the need to establish a leadership structure to give the emerging movement shape and a unified voice.^(31)On October 13–14, the RCCT leadership – representing most of the faiths in the country – duly met at the Machakos Garden Hotel, ostensibly to “pray for the country in the face of Moi and KANU’s hard-line stance.”^(32) The delegates approved the proposed strategy and reinforced the need for a parallel process as a new repertoire of contention in the struggle. The formative framing of contention was that the religious community, besides representing a majority of Kenyans, was also the voice of reason and had the moral authority to take positive redemptive action, in conjunction with the will of the people of Kenya, to improve the country.^(33) The religious leaders saw themselves as “divin[ely] called and [with a] mission which ordains justice and dignity for all men and women [… as well as] morally obligated to proceed with the stalled Constitution Review Process in the Spirit of the Constitution of Kenya Review Act, 1997.”^(34)A key outcome of the Machakos meeting was a resolution to take the process forward in case political parties refused to accede to the seat-sharing formula that the RCCT had suggested.^(35) Thereafter, the RCCT gave the attorney general a one-month ultimatum to convene a consultative forum of stakeholders to confirm its suggested configurations and redress the problems with the act. If the attorney general refused, the RCCT warned, it would take the initiative and convene the forum itself.There was a flurry of activity in the days following the Machakos meeting, including intense consultations among different stakeholders. For example, the religious leaders held further meetings on October 26 and November 2, during which they continued strategizing on how to persuade other stakeholders – specifically political parties and secular civil society – to get on board. Many secular civil society actors as well as most of the opposition political parties agreed to do just that at a preliminary stakeholders’ meeting on December 3, 1999\. Finally, on December 15–16, having received no response to its ultimatum from the attorney general, the RCCT convened a stakeholders’ consultation forum at Ufungamano House. It was this convention that launched the parallel review process.The declaration at the end of the two-day forum announced that the Kenyan people had a sovereign right to draft their own constitution, and that they were now embarking on this mission. Specifically, it stated:**KNOWING** that a constitution is ultimately the expression of the sovereign will of the people.**CONSCIOUS** of the fact, the right and wishes of the people of Kenya to make their own constitution through a people-driven Review Process has been compromised by certain sectors of the political class.**WE** the people of Kenya gathered here at Ufungamano House in Nairobi, under the stewardship of the Religious Community **DO HEREBY LAUNCH** the Constitution of Kenya Review Process by the people of Kenya.The Mechanisms, procedure and timetable of the Review Process will be announced in due course under the guidance of the Steering Committee, which shall include women and Youth.^(36)The Ufungamano Initiative process – despite its lack of legal backing – presented a direct challenge to the legitimacy of the Raila Odinga-led PSC, which the government had formally announced the previous day (December 15, 1999). The announcement of these two parallel processes heightened the stakes, and for a while there seemed to be no turning back for either group. The game moved from accountability politics to each group competing for the support of all Kenyans. Ultimately, the Ufungamano Initiative won the majority of public sympathy, primarily because it was the first constitutional reform initiative in Kenyan history that managed to fashion a national framework that brought together diverse groups. By contrast, the PSC process was seen as an exclusionary ploy by the Moi/KANU regime to deny Kenyans a new constitution.

### The contested mandate of the religious community’s leadership

Despite its success in bringing together diverse constituencies in the push for a people-centred review process, the Ufungamano Initiative was riddled with many contradictions that impacted on its work. As such, while religious leadership came to be seen as “more trusted,” there was only cautious optimism – if not outright hostility – among certain key players, especially the NCCK, the NCEC, and the youth organisations.^(37) The NCCK, which later occupied several pivotal positions in the movement, for instance, initially seemed unwilling to provide the required leadership. This was why the two most prominent Christian forces in Kenya – the NCCK and the Catholic KEC – were both unrepresented at the first meeting, even though they had been invited to attend the crucial October 13–14 Machakos meeting. While Archbishop Njue – chair of the KEC – sent apologies on behalf of the Catholic clergy, the NCCK did not. In fact, it was said that the group’s general secretary – the Rev. Mutava Musyimi – was opposed to the whole idea of a parallel process, and he did not attend any of the meetings that ultimately made it a reality.^(38)This NCCK “boycott” should be understood in the context of the group “defending their turf. The NCCK was used to leading and in particular the general secretary thought that it must be him leading the process.”^(39) Nonetheless, a series of behind-the-scenes negotiations finally persuaded Musyimi to come on board. One delegate at these meetings suggested that he agreed to do so only after the other religious leaders agreed to his demand to steer the process.^(40) Paradoxically, thereafter he became the leading voice of the Ufungamano Initiative. Once again, this demonstrates that personal interests often lay at the heart of these struggles. This notwithstanding, the NCCK’s stance at the formative stage suggests differentiated roles of leadership in the evolution and operations of social movements.The NCEC’s initial discomfort with the religious leaders’ stewardship of the process should also be viewed in the context of hegemonic struggles for control of the reform agenda.^(41) In addition, the centralist tendencies of the clergy may have prompted some groups and individuals to oppose the religious leaders’ stewardship.^(42) Indeed, the clergy’s role, on the whole, was contradictory in many respects. For example, within the Christian community, while the leaders of the mainstream churches (mainly Catholics, Methodists, Anglicans, and Presbyterians) headed the struggle for change, the evangelicals sided with the state. However, this is not to say that there was total unity in the mainstream churches (Mati 2015). Among the Methodists, for instance, one former presiding bishop – Lawi Imathiu – supported Moi and KANU, while the then presiding bishop – Zablon Nthamburi – worked with the rest of the activist clergy in their struggle against the regime.Meanwhile, the youth and student movement challenged the impartiality of the clergy, arguing that every time they had mediated between KANU and other Kenyan interests, the outcome had always been in KANU’s favour.^(43) In response, some members of the clergy accused the most vocal student leaders of being mere “mouthpieces” of activist academics and politicians.^(44) These claims and counter-claims were indicative of the general atmosphere of mistrust and fear of betrayal at the start of the Ufungamano Initiative.In addition, some of the groups allied to the NCEC felt that the religious institutions’ theology was insufficiently radical to motivate and mobilise people to liberate themselves. For instance, the activist Okiya Omtatah argued that “there was no theological exposition of the struggle in the Ufungamano Initiative. It lacked a strong intellectual content, and a theological articulation of the problems.”^(45) This argument has some merit. Had theological questioning of hierarchy and power structures driven the Ufungamano Initiative, a true people’s movement might have developed. Instead, middle-class theologians, secular civil society, and self-serving political activists merely rented subaltern support. In this regard, Rashmin Chitnis from the Hindu Council of Kenya confirmed that his organisation’s involvement in the constitutional reform process was primarily a matter of self-preservation and not transformative in any way.^(46)This admission should come as no surprise, given that, in Kenya, the Hindu community is closely associated with the usually conservative capital-owning class. This is not to suggest that self-preservation is not an important factor in the emergence of social struggles. On the contrary, as Tilly (1995 cited in Buechler 2000: 4) shows, the profound changes in seventeenth- and eighteenth-century Europe were initially met with defensive behaviour by large numbers of ordinary people. Subsequently, offensive popular mobilisations sought to preserve old customs or establish new rights. Of relevance here is that a variety of social formations joined the Ufungamano Initiative for different – and usually self-serving – reasons. For the Kenyan Hindu community, the declining economy, which they attributed to economic mismanagement and poor governance, as well as the turbulent reactions in the form of riots, demonstrations, mass action, and labour unrest, meant dwindling fortunes for its mainly business-oriented membership.^(47) They reacted by joining forces with those who wanted to reverse these trends.Despite the initial scepticism of various stakeholders, the Ufungamano Initiative was possible because Kenya’s religious leaders continued to stress the need for unity if they were to initiate a people-driven review process. By the time of the crystallisation of the initiative, these stakeholders had largely accepted the leadership of the religious community, partly because they were well aware that a segment of activist clergy had been at the heart of the struggle for political reform in Kenya since the late 1980s. Indeed, they had been among the first in the country to oppose the one-party regime (Mati 2015), an act of defiance that endeared them to many Kenyans.In addition, ordinary Kenyans, who were desperate for leadership and guidance on the issue of constitutional reform, trusted the clergy more than any alternative group of leaders. Indeed, one Afrobarometer (2006) survey indicated that Kenyans had more consultations with their religious leaders than they did with politicians and bureaucrats combined.^(48) As such, during the time of the Ufungamano Initiative crystallisation, faith in religious leadership was invoked due to what some paradoxically argued was the clergy’s neutrality and resoluteness. In the formative meeting of December 15–16, 1999, for instance, one leading opposition politician, Kenneth Matiba, argued that religious organisations could “clearly give outlook of the views of Kenyans because they are neutral.”^(49) He stressed that the country’s religious leaders had a mandate from God to rescue Kenyans and were better placed than actors from secular civil society or political parties to steer a people-driven constitutional reform process.At the same meeting, Professor Kivutha Kibwana, an NCEC co-convener, suggested that “all people present [should] be established as stakeholders so that the group can have legal standing … [R]eligious people who are people of God [should] deal with matters of constitutional review meetings since the parliament has not succeeded.”^(50) Dr Gibson Kamau Kuria, another NCEC co-convener, supported this proposal, arguing that the review process needed the sort of direction that religious leaders could provide. This suggests a complete change of position among the NCEC leadership regarding the role of religious leaders. The proposal was debated and agreed through a vote, and a subsequent declaration announced: “[T]his meeting is now the stakeholders’ forum as has been elected until further notice.”^(51) In choosing to follow this path, those present argued that the religious leaders would inspire hope among ordinary Kenyans.In the end, the religious leaders won support by galvanising other actors under the Ufungamano Initiative banner. Although, to an outsider, the initiative’s formation may appear a radical departure from previous attempts at unity, I suggest that it was only possible because it was actually a middle-to-conservative movement led by a clergy that mediated between the extreme radicalism of activist secular civil society and the conservatism of the political elites. Its success in bringing together these opposing forces suggests that broad-based movements are possible when the resultant movement or alliance offers a middle-ground coalition that has the ability to accommodate competing interests and positions.The Ufungamano Initiative, as I illustrate in [Chapter 4](#chapter04.xhtml), was able to challenge and force concessions from a rogue state. These concessions postponed a total meltdown as they forced Moi/KANU to the negotiating table. Nevertheless, the initiative lacked clarity on issues and was beset by “principle-setting challenges.”^(52) This was partly because it brought together various radical and traditionally conservative groups that did not necessarily share a coherent storyline or socioeconomic and political background.Nevertheless, the Ufungamano Initiative entrepreneurs and their followers managed to agree on a clear set of principles for operations and engagement, including: “the process of the constitutional review in Kenya [will be] as important as the eventual content of the constitution and the integrity of the process [will be] central to the legitimacy of the constitution.”^(53) Moreover, it stressed the principles of openness, transparency, inclusivity, accessibility, non-discrimination, accountability, cumulativeness, and self-correction as key and integral to constitution making.^(54) These principles were some of the enduring contributions of the movement to the constitution reform project in Kenya.However, as shall become clear in [Chapter 4](#chapter04.xhtml), primarily because of structural deficiencies, the main actors within the Ufungamano Initiative did not remain entirely faithful to these principles. Moreover, like its predecessors, the Ufungamano Initiative remained mainly urban-based and middle class-led. As such, it is probably most accurately defined as an intra-elite struggle rather than a mass movement. It should not be forgotten that groups such as Muunganao wa Wanavijiji, SEMA, ILISHE, and the Matatu Touts Organisation (MATO), which emerged organically from the grassroots and were driven by people who were directly affected by state oppression, played crucial roles in the contitutional reform process. However, these were all single-issue movements until they linked up with the middle-class-led democratisation struggle and ultimately formed the Ufungamano Initiative.

## Conclusion

Why and how did the Ufungamano Initiative succeed where similar attempts had failed in the past? First, it emerged as a counter-movement to Moi/KANU’s brinkmanship in refusing to allow other actors to participate in a badly mutilated covenant between Kenyans and their governors. The Ufungamano Initiative was the result of a confluence of interests and frustrations after multiple betrayals. It was also a product of various actors and organisations appreciating the benefits of forging a marriage of convenience. Its specific trigger was the stalemate that the Moi/KANU regime engineered by placing bottlenecks to prevent popular participation in the review process.The second factor was political opportunity generated by the efforts of previous struggles and the regime’s repeated failure to keep its promises. There was widespread outrage over the patently anti-democratic state’s refusal to acquiesce to demands for a “people-driven” comprehensive review of its contract with its own citizens. This prompted the mobilisation of a variety of groups and ultimately led to the formation of the Ufungamano Initiative. The emergence of the initiative therefore confirms the centrality of cognitive consciousness in the incubation of protests and struggles, as emphasised by scholars of diverse schools of thought and disciplinary orientations. As Chinua Achebe (1988: 6–7) argues:To answer oppression with appropriate resistance requires knowledge of two kinds. Self-knowledge by the victim means in the first place an awareness that oppression exists … [S]econdly, the victim must know who the enemy is. He must know his oppressor’s real name, not an alias, a pseudonym, or a nom de plume!The Burkinabe revolutionary leader Thomas Sankara (2007: 198) suggests another prerequisite for societal change: “you cannot carry out fundamental change without a certain amount of madness. In this case, it comes from nonconformity, the courage to turn your back on the old formulas, the courage to invent the future.” In the Kenyan case, this consciousness and courage initially emerged in the internecine struggles of the elites and the middle class. However, over time, through a process of advocacy and civic education, it cascaded to the general Kenyan population, resulting in a consensus on the need for reform.That said, different groups of actors continued to disagree over the precise nature and most desirable outcome of such reform. Moreover, as we shall see in [Chapter 4](#chapter04.xhtml), there was a great disconnect between word and deed, especially in the framing of “the people’s agenda” within the Ufungamano Initiative. As such, it was a people’s movement only insofar as it was an alliance of various civil society organisations, religious institutions, political parties, and grassroots movements that shared a desire for constitutional reform. Long-standing fragmentations among elite groups continued in the constitutional reform process, so much so that it is credible to conclude that politicians – whether in opposition or in power – based their support or opposition to reform primarily on calculations of the extent to which this would advance or reduce their own chances of seizing or retaining power. They wished to protect or enhance their own political and economic interests, so the reform process was never likely to have significant emancipatory or transformative capacity (Nzomo 2003).This chapter has also argued that, despite the centrality of the reformers’ shared grievances and cognitive consciousness, such factors cannot generate social movements on their own. Another crucial ingredient is the leadership of entrepreneurs who “can channel their moral and philosophical worldviews and deeply felt convictions to stop threatening developments, redress instances of injustice, promote alternative options to the managing of social life and economic activity” (della Porta and Diani 2006 [1999]: 3).[Chapter 4](#chapter04.xhtml) analyses the contradictions in form (structure), operational frame, and strategy that ultimately defined the effectiveness and limits of the Ufungamano Initiative.Notes1    M.
