# [4    Leadership, framing, and mobilisation strategies](#toc.xhtml_ch-04)

## Introduction

The Ufungamano Initiative focused fundamentally on the democratic participation of Kenyan citizens in the process of drafting a new constitution. However, at the same time, it failed to implement full democratic participation and accountability in its own ranks. This chapter analyses the tensions between democratic claims and democratic weaknesses within the Ufungamano Initiative. Specifically, it explores the initiative’s issue-framing and mobilisation structures with a view to showing how tensions between democratic claims and democratic deficiencies emerged and were reproduced. The chapter places the circumstances of the initiative’s emergence and its leadership at the heart of the decisions that led to the development and reproduction of anti-democratic tendencies within the movement and its relationship with the Kenyan public. It suggests that the Ufungamano Initiative had multiple internal contradictions, especially between its rhetoric when framing contention as a campaign for a people-driven constitutional reform process and its practice. The chapter argues that these contradictions were products of fragmentations emanating from a heterogeneity of actors and interests as well as the movement’s mobilisation structures and strategies. Specifically, the Ufungamano Initiative embodied three contradictory tensions: the necessity to mediate between diverse forces; the need to engage in symbolic and negotiating levels of struggle; and the desire to perpetuate elite domination in the midst of an increasingly empowered subaltern class. This called for the adoption of structures that would allow the movement to navigate these contradictions.The chapter therefore demonstrates that, despite the Ufungamano Initiative’s insistence that constitution making is enshrined in Kenyan citizens’ fundamental right to direct democratic participation, its internal practices, decision-making power, and structures did not guarantee the maximisation of popular participation. This was because, rather than ensuring sufficient dispersal of authority, decision-making power was concentrated in a few leaders who enjoyed first-among-equals status within the movement. The chapter therefore aligns with Marx’s criticism of deficiencies in the actualisation of liberal democracy’s “language of equality, solidarity, emancipation, and the transformations of existing power relations” (cited in Held and McGrew 2003: 114).The chapter is organised into five main sections. The first of these introduces the general analytical frame that leadership was key in the action phase of the movement. The next two analyse the leadership’s role in strategic decision-making and empowering followers, respectively. The fourth explores how the Ufungamano Initiative framed its demands and its strategies for contention. The fifth main section explores the initiative’s institutional and organisational structures and how these related to one another to ensure upward, horizontal, and downward accountability within the movement. Here, the critical question is: did these structures aid or hinder the Ufungamano Initiative’s work? This section also addresses the relationship between the leadership and the structural context in relation to mobilising capacities and resources to steer the movement towards its goals. Finally, a brief conclusion summarises the chapter’s arguments.

## Leadership in the Ufungamano Initiative’s action phase

A movement’s leadership is key in framing demands, determining repertoires of action and strategies, inspiring commitment among followers, mobilising resources, and creating and recognising opportunities, which in turn influence outcomes, successes, and failures (Morris and Staggenborg 2004). Leadership roles may metamorphose and diffuse among a movement’s various levels, members, and structures. The diffusion of leadership ensures that roles and responsibilities are spread to many members. Such dispersal allows for greater influence and more roles for various members of the movement. There was considerable diffusion within the Ufungamano Initiative:One could say that the religious leaders who were the co-chairs were leaders. But then, within the leadership, there were so many components reflective of the many important things that needed to be done so that things could go on. Therefore, maybe the leadership was embodied in the people that defined the agenda, because there were many leaders at different levels. So, to define a movement’s leadership, one has to look at what kind of influence different actors had within the movement.¹The Ufungamano Initiative narrative suggests that different leaders emerged at different stages in the movement’s development to perform different roles. As such, those who played a leading role in incubating the movement were not necessarily the leaders during the action phase. That said, a number of actors performed a variety of leadership roles either concurrently or at different stages in the movement’s evolution.There were at least four leadership functions in the Ufungamano Initiative: first, strategic decision-making (including creation and/or recognition and utilisation of political opportunities) in the emergence and action phases; second, empowering and inspiring followers; third, framing demands and strategies for contention and mobilisation; and, finally, determining mobilising structures. The distinctions between these functions were rather fuzzy, and there were several overlaps. For example, the first and third functions both entailed the formulation of effective strategies. The remainder of this chapter is devoted to discussing these four functions in detail.

## Leadership and strategic decision-making

As already noted in [Chapter 3](#chapter03.xhtml), Kenya’s religious leaders were the entrepreneurs who mobilised others to establish the Ufungamano Initiative in response to the Moi–Odinga axis’s intransigence against popular participation in the constitutional reform process. As such, from the very beginning, the launch of a parallel process by the Ufungamano Initiative was a strategic decision that ensued from lengthy and skilful stakeholder consultations and deliberations spearheaded by the RCCT at the instigation of the Kenya Women’s Political Caucus.Detailed analysis of the steps and processes leading up to the adoption of this parallel process (see [Chapter 3](#chapter03.xhtml)) suggests that skilful and strategic leadership was essential in realising that the politics of the street (i.e. mass protests) for which the NCEC-allied organisations had been calling was unable to break the stalemate that the political elites had deliberately created. In consequence, innovative ideas were needed to apply pressure and coerce the state into some sort of compromise.²This strategic decision-making required clarity of ideas and an ability to recognise and create opportunities for action. This was exemplified by the role played by the leadership of different stakeholders during the Safari Park negotiations between May and October 1998\. While these meetings were not explicitly framed as political opportunities at the time, when read in conjunction with developments in 1999 that emphasised fidelity to the Safari Park and previous IPPG agreements in the face of Moi’s obstinate resistance to a people-centred review process, it is evident that they were just that. This is because the Ufungamano Initiative entrepreneurs utilised Moi/KANU’s infidelity to the agreements to incubate a movement and frame contention. As a former People’s Commission of Kenya (PCK) and later Constitution of Kenya Review Commission (CKRC) commissioner pointed out, after Moi’s May 1999 pronouncement in which he reneged on the promises contained within the 1997 Constitution of Kenya Review Act, other stakeholders realised that there would be no progress.³ From then on, Ufungamano Initiative entrepreneurs returned to the very act that many had previously dismissed as unworkable as they saw that it provided an opportunity to mobilise for accountability: “all we wanted to do now was just go on and implement the act, collect people’s views and come up with a draft constitution as the act had stipulated.”⁴More political opportunities resulted from the changes that were taking place globally at the time. Demands for a politics of accountability and transparency and multi-partyism were supported by readily available cash from Western donors channelled to NGOs, especially in the context of the crisis of the confidence in the state within the dominant neoliberal order of the time. In this regard, one of the key players the Ufungamano Initiative indicated:Because a majority of us in the Ufungamano Initiative were not your orthodox religious organisations, but rather quasi-religious NGOs, we knew NGO-speak; we knew the NGO culture. So we wrote proposals and because of the language and [the fact that] the demands could be paid for, it became easy for us to mobilise for donor funds for some of the activities that we wanted to do.⁵Moreover, at the time, there was a wind of change blowing across the African continent as a number of dictatorial regimes were either swept from power or forced to adapt to a new democratic and/or multi-party constitutional order.⁶ These new constitutional dispensations across many Sub-Saharan African countries served as reference points for the Ufungamano Initiative. For instance, one activist recalled:South Africa had just enacted a constitution that almost became a blueprint for a number of countries, especially due to the fact that it had eventually gone beyond entrenching civil and political rights to [include] socioeconomic rights … It became an inspiration. A lot of constitutions that followed tended to borrow a lot from the South African 1996 constitutional framework.⁷Ufungamano Initiative leaders also utilised international human rights conventions that Kenya had signed but was doing little to implement as further opportunities to push for accountability.⁸ The main tactic here was to highlight the discrepancy between the regime’s international obligations and the domestic situation.Another opportunity that was exploited to bring together disparate opposition groups was the fact that they shared a common adversary – the Moi/KANU regime. One Catholic theologian offered an insight into why the reformists needed to exploit this, arguing that many groups had campaigned for a long time against the Moi/KANU regime’s excesses before realising that there was no way they would win if they continued to act independently of one another:It was very easy to be isolated because the state would frame your resistance as “Catholics or Protestants are against this or that.” So it was a matter of saying, “Here is a common problem; a problem that affects the nation. It is a cry for basic rights, for food, for clothing, for the country’s wealth.” So, a collective national effort was required. And, because Moi had become very powerful and involved in a lot of impunity, he would hit back at any opposition either by killing or by bribing. But acting collectively, we realised there was no way they could kill everybody as the outcry would be too much.⁹The 1997 general election, in which Moi/KANU lost a number of parliamentary seats, generated further opportunities. Suddenly, “Moi looked ‘beatable.’ Ordinary Kenyans saw the Ufungamano movement as having a lot of hope of delivering them from Moi’s malaise”^(10) This explains why the movement emerged when it did: more of the Kenyan people now believed that Moi and KANU could be successfully challenged.The opportunities highlighted above point to the fact that an existing order becomes an opportunity only when leaders make strategic choices to utilise it to incubate and drive contention. Indeed, as Morris and Staggenborg (2004: 171) argue, “movement leaders [are] strategic decision-makers who inspire and organize others to participate in social movement.” After hatching, a movement moves into an action phase, when it is steered by cadres who translate general discontent into comprehensible terms for the distressed masses and inspire commitment to collective action.^(11) In the Ufungamano Initiative, this happened in a hostile political environment. Successful navigation of these hostilities depended on the leadership’s ability to guide operations by creating structures for mobilisation and support while also framing demands to make the movement attractive to a wider audience. It is to these aspects that this chapter now turns.

## Leadership and the empowerment of followers

One of the Ufungamano Initiative leadership’s principal strategies to win support and inspire followers was to empower Kenyans through awareness-raising citizens’ rights education. Such empowerment enabled some of the masses to “move from a position of total subservience – accepting everything because they could not confront power – to a level where people were able to stand up, question power, and demand change.”^(12)But how exactly did this empowerment happen? Within the Ufungamano Initiative’s structure, a formal committee responsible for “civic education” was formed. Civic education entailed raising the political awareness of the people, which resulted in demands for greateraccountability and government policies and acts were subjected to greater scrutiny … it gave individuals, organized groups, and communities the incentive to study the ways in which public power can be organized and exercised. It increased their awareness of the structures of state.(Cottrell and Ghai 2007: 23)Civic education had a number of impacts on this struggle. First, it politicized subaltern struggles by making the masses aware of the fact that the problems they faced were rooted in a bad constitution.^(13) As a result, subalterns joined hands with the middle class to resist exploitation (Cottrell and Ghai 2007).Secondly, civic education was an important tool for bridging grassroots and national struggles. In this regard, incentives for alliances between the different struggles had to be carefully framed. These came in the form of support (especially financial resources and *pro bono* legal assistance) for subaltern groups such as Muungano wa Wanavijiji from middle-class-led NGOs such as Kituo cha Sheria.^(14) This illustrates the emergence of alliances between the middle and lower classes, which was a significant development that encouraged the masses to continue their protests. It was a symbiotic relationship that benefited both the subaltern movements and the middle-class organisations:There were many restrictions, which made our work difficult. But the support we got from NGOs … helped us … We did not have wealth, but we had good imagination … We joined these struggles because we wanted our voices as ordinary citizens to be heard alongside others agitating for change in the country … [T]heir struggles also resonated with our struggles. We helped them build their legitimacy because they had people and movements like ours in them.^(15)Such support made the politicisation of subaltern struggles possible and alliances with middle-class struggles attractive. Furthermore, the interventions of these NGOs through civic education and solidarity (for instance, participation in joint demonstrations) encouraged grassroots campaigners to join the largely middle-class struggle for constitutional reform.^(16)Civic education also displayed aspects of diagnostic framing by highlighting weaknesses in the constitution as well as discrepancies between the government’s constitutional responsibilities and actions. By participating in civic education forums, members of subaltern groups interacted with middle-class experts and gained knowledge that cascaded to their communities and proved useful for accountability politics.^(17) In this regard, Ezekiel Rema, a leader of Muungano Wa Wanavijiji, stated:We got skills that we used to create more awareness for people and tell them, “This is what the constitution of Kenya says about land.” We were able to talk confidently in our forums. On the issue of housing, it was very clear international treaties had been signed by government; we knew that there was a convention where the government committed themselves that they would deliver this and that, and were not delivering. Such knowledge added a greater force to our struggle. In that process we were also able to interact with many powerful people who helped us by participating in our demonstrations.^(18)Such interactions brought to the fore a third outcome of civic education. Specifically, solidarities were built across the board that helped middle-class reformers to “build their own legitimacy because they had ordinary people’s movements like Muungano wa Wanavijiji with them.”^(19) This was because framing processes involved claim making, and such claims needed validity. As a leader of one of the middle-class NGOs that provided support to subaltern struggles noted, “Moi kept asking the question: ‘Who do you represent?’ There needed to be an answer to Moi’s question.”^(20) This answer came in the form of subaltern struggles being “asked to get expression in the constitutional reform movement.”^(21) As such, Muungano wa Wanavijiji’s positive interactions with middle-class human rights NGOs suggests some inter-class interdependence in these struggles.The fourth product of civic education was the creation of widespread awareness. Citizens were so well informed about their rights that many of them had already adopted firm positions by the time that the PCK and CKRC travelled around the country to collect their views, so the level of manipulation was minimal. This widespread awareness was a result of the cumulative impact of interdependent interactions that began in the early 1990s, with groups such as the 4Cs sensitising the masses on the need to overhaul the constitution. This sensitisation made the masses “yearn for reforms because most were affected by adverse conditions in the country.”^(22)The fifth impact of civic education was to strengthen Kenyans’ resolve and commitment to “ameliorative collective action” (Benford and Snow 2000: 617). Accordingly, some have argued that this resolve ultimately led to citizens pushing for a new constitution as the solution to all of Kenya’s problems.Clearly, civic education served more than immediate empowerment and framing goals. The impacts enumerated above were not accidental, but the results of strategic calculations on the part of leadership. In this regard, Mutava Musyimi, a leading member of the movement who ultimately became its spokesperson, stated: “we believed that it was right that people be helped to understand why we were doing what we were doing.”^(23) The Ufungamano Initiative leadership was therefore acutely aware of the need to convince Kenyans that the contention would result in better lives for all. This was especially so because, as Rashmin Chitnis, the chair of the Hindu Council of Kenya, argued, due to the prevailing political environment,Most people had a resigned feeling that nothing would make a difference. However, some of the people did have some hope that constitutional reforms would eventually bring institutional reforms. But this was going to be a slow process that needed patience and longer-term commitment.^(24)In January 2000, Kivutha Kibwana, a leading member of the NCEC and its spokesperson, sent a memorandum to the Ufungamano Initiative. This captured the issues with which the initiative had to grapple and the importance of remaining committed to the cause:If the Ufungamano Initiative stands firm, the Raila Odinga–Moi route of constitutional reform will not succeed. It will not accrue the necessary countrywide legitimacy … However, if they waiver, MPs currently not supporting the parliamentary initiative may trickle back to Raila.Furthermore, to counter continued attacks by the state’s propaganda machinery on the legality of their process, the Ufungamano Initiative leadership argued that, while the government process was legal according to the letter of the law, it did not enjoy the legitimate support of the Kenyan people. By contrast,The Ufungamano Initiative had moral authority; Kenyans wanted a constitutional reform process that was people driven. Religious leaders, like Mutava Musyimi, kept on reminding us that we were the moral voice of this country and had to give guidance to this country.^(25)Besides, the Ufungamano Initiative leadership could frame their struggle in theological terms. Specifically, some religious leaders argued that it was their duty to get involved in political matters to save the country. They saw their mission on earth as not just spiritual but political, social, and economic. As such, when the government failed, religious institutions had a role to play in forcing the state to respond to citizens’ demands. One religious leader recalled:There was a universal spirituality present in all religions, whether Muslims or Hindus or Buddhists or Christians or African indigenous religions. They all aim at that peace and harmony that the Creator wanted. So religion is more of a vehicle to a higher human goal. And that was the goal that was being attacked from its roots by the Moi regime. When you are preaching every day to people that they should not kill, and murders by the state still increase, you feel threatened.^(26)As such, the leaders of the various faith groups reached out to each other because they were spiritually convinced that they should rise to the occasion and do something positive for the country.With this framing, the Ufungamano Initiative leadership motivated and inspired its followers. Whatever resistance and harassment they faced from the state, they countered by promising Kenyans that they would not be deflected from pursuing change. They kept on reminding the country that it was the regime that had reneged on earlier agreements, so it was necessary for other Kenyans to do everything in their power to get the process back on track, put an end to the wanton loss of human life and property, and get the nation working again by addressing the collapsed economy, deepening official graft, and poverty.^(27)Despite evidence of theological framing, there were some contradictions in the nature of the movement. Some participants have even questioned whether the Ufungamano Initiative had an authentic liberation theology orientation. They claimed that such a movement would have remained steadfast in its demand for a new constitution and would have established participatory and emancipatory structures to ensure genuine mass participation. Instead, the struggle remained largely a battle between rival elite factions. Okiya Omtatah highlighted these contradictions when he stated:It is true the clergy had issues that they tried to address. But they were not tactical; they were not radical enough … But it did provide a platform from which some demands were made on the rogue state, and they were able to force concessions that avoided a meltdown … They managed to bring us to a talking table of sorts … But it did not go far enough because it lacked clarity on issues. And that comes out when Moi goes and Kibaki comes in and the identities change. You find that Catholics now feel, because Kibaki is a Catholic like them, they do not have to work against the government. So, were they fighting Moi and his identity or were they fighting for an ideal? So, you find that they were fighting against the oppression, yes, but with no ideals to replace the problem they were dealing with.^(28)There is some merit in this criticism. As will become clear in [Chapter 5](#chapter05.xhtml), the clergy led the Ufungamano Initiative to a merger that greatly compromised the chance of achieving radical reform. This merger was possible, in part, because the movement relied heavily on the religious institutions’ existing structures, which meant that these institutions enjoyed a disproportionate amount of influence (see below).Nonetheless, I believe that Omtatah is overly critical, not least because he ignores the fact that the clergy-led Ufungamano Initiative succeeded where most previous struggles had failed. Moreover, he fails to acknowledge the multiple nodes of leadership within the movement, choosing, instead, to heap blame on the clergy for its ideological indolence. Yes, the clergy were the most visible members of the leadership. But the critical question is: why did other actors not push the movement in the direction they wanted? Omtatah’s criticism also betrays a valorised conception of the role of civil society. Specifically, he treads a slippery path that leads to a conception of African civil society as always in opposition to – and hoping to transform – the state. His charge of changing identities after 2002 brings out the complex and fluid nature of state–civil society relations in Kenya (see below). However, he fails to recognise the means and ends congruence/divergence as key in determining the nature of these relations. Nor is the changing nature of the political economy taken into account when explaining the changing positions and identities.Despite Omtatah’s critique, the Ufungamano Initiative was able to mobilise support, commitment, and resources from many Kenyans that ensured an energised, solid force and, for once, what appeared to be a unified push for a new constitutional dispensation. Most of the groups that opposed state hegemony and demanded a new constitution congregated within the Ufungamano Initiative’s constitution-making project. Ordinary Kenyans on urban streets and in village squares, churches, mosques, and temples accepted and identified with the alternative vision that the initiative offered. Mutava Musyimi captured this when he argued that there was “public admiration” for the movement: “I get the impression we had massive public support for the process because, even financially, we raised money from the public.”^(29) Musyimi felt that the public liked the idea of a protest movement led by the religious community, which was viewed as fairly stable, reasonable, and strongly opposed to violence, in contrast to the militancy of the NCEC street protests of 1997, which the Ufungamano Initiative shunned. This suggests that at least some of the movement’s appeal lay in its non-violent orientation.^(30) Musyimi’s assertions are supported by the fact that many ordinary Kenyans participated in Ufungamano Initiative activities not directly but through its constituent religious organisations.Despite the generally positive response among many Kenyans, others displayed mixed feelings – if not open hostility – towards the Ufungamano Initiative, especially in Luo Nyanza. In part, this was driven by ethnic politics, but other contributory factors were resentment over the movement’s top-down organisation, its relationships with affiliate groups, and the behaviour of the elite. Specifically, the avenues for participation were usually more accessible to members of the elite and the middle class. This deterred some potential supporters, who felt slighted, and gave ammunition to the KANU and the NDP. For instance, when Ufungamano Initiative commissioners attended a public hearing in Kisumu, a militia group allied to the NDP – the Baghdad Boys – attempted to petrol-bomb the event. Such hostile reactions reflect intra-elite power struggles for control of the reform process and subalterns.Audi Oganda, a one-time leader of the Baghdad Boys, confirmed that the attack was part of an ongoing battle for political supremacy between Raila Odinga and James Orengo in Luo Nyanza.^(31) Odinga and Orengo are prominent members of the Luo community, who in the early 1990s belonged to so-called “Young Turks” in Ford Kenya, then led by Jaramogi Oginga Odinga (Raila Odinga’s father). Odinga Senior died in 1995 and Raila campaigned to succeed him as leader of Ford Kenya. Orengo, who at the time was vice-chair of the party, supported a rival candidate, Kijana Wamalwa, an ethnic Luhya. Raila Odinga lost an acrimonious leadership election that was marred by violence and bloodshed, and ultimately left Ford Kenya for the little-known NDP in 1997\. He used his new party to campaign for the presidency in the 1997 general election, finishing third. Thereafter, his alliance with Moi surprised many, not least because the regime had previously detained him without trial for a total of more than seven years. It was this that led to his appointment as chair of the PSC in December 1999.While Odinga continued to cooperate with KANU, Orengo headed Muungano wa Mageuzi, which was affiliated to the Ufungamano Initiative. The chair of the initiative’s PCK – Dr Oki Ombaka – also happened to be an ethnic Luo and a former Ford Kenya parliamentarian. Hence, it seems that the Ufungamano Initiative was targeted in Kisumu not so much because it was opposed to the state-led reform process – which by then was chaired by Odinga – but specifically to embarrass Orengo and Ombaka in front of the Luo Nyanza people, a majority of whom supported the Odinga family, given Jaramogi Oginga Odinga’s pivotal role in independence and post-independence struggles and his son’s role in more recent pro-democracy campaigns.Several sources concurred with this narrative of ethnic mobilisation against a popular campaign. Gideon Ochanda, for instance, stated: “The PCK commissioners were on the other side … In Kisumu, demonstrators were ‘owned’ by Raila and if you had opposing views, the public would throw stones at you. That is what they did to Oki Ombaka on that day.”^(32) On the other hand, Ochieng Khairallah advanced an alternative theory:The powers that be were very impatient and felt threatened by the activities of the Ufungamano Initiative because it had very broad and popular support among the masses, who felt the Ufungamano Initiative articulated their collective aspirations. People seemed to say: “In the Ufungamano Initiative, we see ourselves out of the problems that have for a long time afflicted the very heart and the very soul of society.”^(33)Meanwhile, Samuel Nyabinda, a former councillor in Kisumu, suggested yet another reason for the hostile reception. For him, the locals resented the presence of a group of commissioners who, they felt, delayed real change and frustrated the will of the people:We had the second liberation heroes and some of the people in the Ufungamano Initiative had not been part of it. Many people could not understand how, now, the Ufungamano Initiative could change the system whereas they had not done it when we wanted the repeal of Section 2A. It was not just [an attack against] the Ufungamano commissioners here in Kisumu; it was their delaying of the process that we attacked.^(34)Nyabinda added that he witnessed the NDP talking to KANU, and saw agreements between the two parties to deliver a new constitution that reflected a change of heart within the regime.However, it is difficult to sustain Nyabinda’s argument, given Moi/KANU’s determination to thwart the Ufungamano Initiative. Moreover, the myopic and utilitarian view of power among Kenyan politicians, including Raila Odinga, negates the claim that the hostile reception in Kisumu was a protest against the Ufungamano Initiative’s supposed reform-delaying credentials. Specifically, Odinga’s own actions in leading his party into an alliance and eventual merger with KANU, and his complicity in derailing the reform train, betray other intentions. Therefore, it is difficult to see how any progressive change would have emerged from the Moi–Odinga axis, given that they had shown little interest in anything but the retention of power and dominance.These conflicting interpretations notwithstanding, there was genuine disquiet even among those who supported the Ufungamano Initiative in Kisumu. This was because the meeting was organised from Nairobi by Chemi Chemi ya Ukweli youths. Understandably, local supporters in Kisumu felt slighted by this and wondered why they could not be trusted to organise the event themselves. To some extent, such decisions undermined the legitimacy of the Ufungamano Initiative at the local level.^(35) Ultimately, though, as the whole of Kenya highly charged and state intimidation was rife, the leadership felt it had no option but to leave organisational matters in the hands of a group it knew it could trust. Consequently, notwithstanding the attack in Kisumu and a similarly hostile reception in Garissa in north-eastern Kenya that was attributed to vested elite interests, the movement’s commissioners generally received a warm welcome in the places they visited.

## Leadership and the framing of demands and strategies for contention

Another key role of leaders was to shape demand frames and strategies for contention. Through such framing and strategies, the Ufungamano Initiative captured the popular support that was necessary to challenge the state-run process. As a result, the state was forced to seek ways to save face in light of a growing legitimacy crisis for its own constitutional review process. Key to the legitimacy of the Ufungamano Initiative was its framing of contention around the constitution-drafting process.Keck and Sikkink (1998) argue that, for the framing of demands to be effective, it must be clear, dramatic, powerful, and appeal to shared principles. For Keck and Sikkink (1998: 20), effective framing “calls attention to issues, or creates them by using language that dramatizes and draws attention to their concerns.” They identify four key strategies that movements use for the purposes of framing:

1.  Information politics, wherein movements quickly and credibly generate politically usable information and use it where it has most impact.
2.  Symbolic politics, which entails movements identifying and providing convincing explanations for powerful symbolic events.
3.  Leverage politics, which entails identifying targets that may be held up to the scrutiny of peers, thereby exerting moral leverage on the assumption that governments or other targets value others’ opinions.
4.  Accountability politics, in which movements try to convince their targeted actors to change their public positions on cetain issues. This is done by exposing any differences between the talk and the walk.

All four of these strategies were evident in the Ufungamano Initiative to varying degrees. Initially, even before the launch of its parallel process, Ufungamano Initiative entrepreneurs styled themselves as a pressure group to force the state to acquiesce over what they framed as the Kenyan people’s fundamental and sovereign right to decide the way in which they wanted to be governed. Hence, the entrepreneurs argued that the constitutional reform process should be people-centred, and the commission steering the process had to include a variety of social groups. Subsequently, the Ufungamano Initiative justified this framing by arguing that the people were superior to state institutions:Constitution making is a social, rather than a legal process. Therefore, parliament cannot engage in this process without violating the constitution … Parliamentary intermeddling only makes the process unconstitutional … [O]nly Kenyans acting collectively as a people are above the constitution and therefore have the sovereign right to act outside the constitution while making a new constitution. No institution that is a creature of the constitution can act outside the constitution. It is not therefore any individual or group of individuals that wish to lock Parliament, the Presidency or the Attorney General out of the constitution making process – it is the current constitution itself that locks them out. The occupiers of the offices of the President, Attorney General, and Members of Parliament can only participate in this process as individuals like other ordinary Kenyans.^(36)Therefore, for the Ufungamano Initiative, the power of all three arms of government (judiciary, parliament, and executive) lay beneath and depended on the mandate of the Kenyan people. As such, the superiority of parliament in the process of constitution making did not arise because parliament could exercise its legislative responsibility only within the parameters of the constitution, not outside. Here, we see the Ufungamano Initiative utilising the same constitution it wished to rewrite in order to challenge the actions of its rivals. It did this by appropriating the “We, the people” tag and promised to carry out the review process with all other Kenyans.But how, precisely, did the Ufungamano Initiative utilise information, symbolic, leverage, and accountability politics? The following sub-sections provide an explanation.

### Symbolic politics

The Ufungamano Initiative framed the constitution as a sacred covenant (symbolic politics) between the Kenyan people and their rulers that required input from both parties. As the former head of the Catholic Church in Kenya, Archbishop Ndingi Mwana a’Nzeki, put it: “the constitution belongs to all Kenyans and not just to the political class in parliament” (cited in *Weekly Review* 1999: 8).^(37) Several key figures belaboured this point during the December 15–16 meeting that formally launched the movement. The spokesperson for the NCEC, Kivutha Kibwana, for example, called for the rejection of the Parliamentary Select Committee, which was in competition with the Ufungamano Initiative, as it was an illegal body under the terms of the current constitution. A leading female politician, Charity Ngilu (the first woman to campaign for the presidency and subsequently leader of the Social Democratic Party), eloquently argued that Kenya belonged to all and that they would not allow parliament to abrogate the ultimate power of the Kenyan people. Such framing in a political environment hostile to the same confirms the nature of civil society as a site of resistance to hegemonic power. Such sites of resistance are important social imaginaries for alternatives.

### Information politics

Information politics were important in generating legitimacy for the movement’s leadership and its demands. As already pointed out, the movement achieved this through civic education of the masses. As shall become clear later in this chapter, a specific organ within the Ufungamano Initiative was tasked with civic education. Another manifestation of information politics was through detailed analysis of the law. Such analysis of the 1997 Constitution of Kenya Review Act enabled actors within the Ufungamano Initiative to identify political and legal opportunities as well as “flaws.”Information politics were also evident in messages that capitalised on the anxieties created by Moi/KANU’s refusal to accommodate other stakeholders. The argument was that Kenyans had placed immense faith in the envisaged process and that, by reneging on earlier promises, the regime was igniting chaos. To get this message across, the Ufungamano Initiative relied heavily on existing independent media houses like Royal Media Services, the religious organisations’ own communication channels and media, and independent newspapers and magazines. Together with the emergent cellular phone system, these were critical avenues for the mobilisation and marshalling of support, as the state-controlled public broadcaster – the Kenya Broadcasting Corporation – remained hostile to the Ufungamano Initiative. This suggests that the liberalisation of the Kenyan airwaves from the early 1990s presented the movement with an important political opportunity. Its standing with the Kenyan public might have been very different had it emerged when the state broadcaster enjoyed a monopoly.A former PCK commissioner explained that they reached out strategically to editors in various independent media houses and explained that the Ufungamano Initiative was not anarchist, despite the government’s efforts to portray it as such. On the contrary, they merely wanted what was best for the country. From then on, independent media organisations took them seriously, reported their work, and sometimes even criticised the state when it tried to suppress their activities: “When, for example, we were attacked in Kisumu, the print media put it as front-page story. It also castigated these goons as well as their paymasters.”^(38)Nonetheless, such gestures from the media houses were always dependent on their ownership.^(39) There were many instances of selective coverage as journalists and their editors focused on which reports would sell the most papers, rather than the merits of the struggle. This selectivity resulted in marginalisation of the agenda of “non-prominent citizens.” As such, the nature of the media’s role has been contested. While those who controlled the Ufungamano Initiative believed that the media supported their struggle, those at the bottom of the power pyramid felt otherwise. These disagreements suggest that the media was more concerned with – or more sensitive to pressure from – the dominant socioeconomic classes:Our media is pro-status quo. It values and promotes interests of dominant groups. If you talk to any journalist who has been to the school of journalism, they tell you that the number one news value is prominence: i.e. how prominent is the person speaking? So, if [Morris] Odhiambo is speaking somewhere and [Vice-President] Kalonzo Musyoka is somewhere nearby, Odhiambo never gets noticed. And this is Odhiambo, the chief executive of CLARION. Imagine the man or woman sitting at the street corner, a vendor, or a hawker … They are totally marginalised.^(40)Therefore, if the Ufungamano Initiative had been an ordinary citizens’ struggle, it may not have received nearly as much media coverage as it did, which would have impacted its ability to use information politics to endear itself to the Kenyan people.

### Leverage politics

Leverage politics were discernible in the formation the People’s Commission of Kenya (PCK) – the technical arm of the Ufungamano Initiative that was tasked with leading the Kenyan people in drafting their own constitution when it became apparent that KANU would never allow popular participation. It became the most potent of the movement’s strategies despite initial concerns over the viability of an alternative review process.^(41) By launching a parallel commission that was independent of the state at a time when KANU’s fidelity to the 1997 Constitution of Kenya Review Act was in question, the Ufungamano Initiative managed to pose the greatest credibility and legitimacy challenge to the parliament-led process. In this regard, P.L.O. Lumumba, who became secretary of the Constitution of Kenya Review Commission (CKRC) after the merger of the two processes, observed: “While the process was led by parliament and predicated upon a piece of legislation, it was heavily challenged. The Ufungamano Initiative enjoyed a lot of legitimacy among Kenyans.”^(42) Arguably, were it not for the PCK, the Ufungamano Initiative, like so many of its predecessors, may have been totally ignored. But the alternative power that it wielded ensured its relevance.

### Accountability politics

The Ufungamano Initiative’s use of accountability politics revolved around the government’s lack of fidelity to the IPPG, Bomas, and Safari Park agreements, the subsequent 1998 Constitution of Kenya Review Commission Amendment Act, and the various international human rights conventions to which Kenya was a signatory (see above). Specifically, the movement called upon the Moi/KANU regime to provide evidence of its efforts to adhere to the treaties it had signed and/or ratified.However, the various groups within the Ufungamano Initiative utilised accountability politics in different ways, which steered the movement in different directions and ultimately diluted its agenda. More fundamentally, this exposed the recurring ideational polarities within the movement. One faction insisted that the Ufungamano Initiative process should be entirely divorced from the 1997 Review Act, while another felt that it should work towards implementing the Act’s mandate, irrespective of its flaws. Those opposed to the Act argued:It lacked the legal, social and political legitimacy because the Act was *ultra vires* and with no support or basis in the Constitution … [and was] anchored on no known section of the Constitution. There [was] no provision in the Constitution under which the Act [could] be made [because] the Constitution did not provide for its review, and any Act of Parliament that presuppose[d] to legislate for a process of the review of the Constitution [was] therefore unconstitutional, null and void.^(43)There was also some evidence of accountability politics in the creation of structures to boost participation. To a great extent, the Ufungamano Initiative operated an open-door policy that ensured that all reform-minded activists and groups (social, cultural, economic, and political) were welcomed. In the words of Timothy Njoya, this strategy was employed because it is the cardinal business of religious leaders to deliver all souls to righteousness.^(44) As such, the Ufungamano Initiative embraced anyone who wished to take part in the process.Having dealt with leadership and its role in strategic decision-making and the framing of contention, the crucial question is: which structures did the movement establish to ensure that it achieved what it set out to do? The answer to this question helps to explain the interplay between the various forces within the Ufungamano Initiative and provides an insight into the movement’s strengths and weaknesses. Moreover, it provides a material basis for understanding leadership, framing, and strategies.

## The Ufungamano Initiative’s mobilising structures

Structures, strategies, and resources are the levers that drive a movement’s work. As Tarrow (1998 [1994]: 123) points out, structures are the resources that allow contentious acts to be sustained as movements because they “bring people together, shape coalitions, confront opponents, and assure their own future after the exhilaration of the peak of mobilization has passed.” Therefore, they are both the engines and the oil that sustain a movement’s actions (Mati 2008). Nonetheless, the precise nature of those structures depends on the issue(s) as well as the diversity of the movement’s constituent parts.Within the Ufungamano Initiative, proposals advanced by one of its constituent parts – the Stakeholders’ Support Group – suggest that the movement was acutely aware of the need for concrete structures. Consequently, a clear strategic framework to facilitate the establishment of various organs was put in place. This framework largely borrowed from the objectives and spirit of the 1997 Review Act. The preamble to the framework read:**We** members of the Ufungamano Forum: **Recognizing** that an open, accountable and democratic system of government that promotes the respect for human rights and sustained economic and social development is a desire of the Kenyan People; **Having** regard to the valiant efforts and painful sacrifices that have been made by the people of Kenya, to establish such a system of government since the dawn of colonial invasion; **Considering** that a national consensus has emerged on the need to comprehensively review the Constitution of Kenya through an open, inclusive and participatory process as a critical measure in the establishment of a just and enduring democratic society; **Recalling** the numerous breaches of promises committed by the Government with regard to constitutional reforms; **Determined** to secure for ourselves and for the future generations a just, peaceful and prosperous society, **DO HEREBY ESTABLISH THIS FRAMEWORK FOR THE COMPREHENSIVE REVIEW OF THE CONSTITUTION OF KENYA BY THE PEOPLE OF KENYA**. ^(45)The December 15–16, 1999 meeting (hereafter Ufungamano I Plenary) therefore attempted to give shape to the Ufungamano Initiative’s nascent review process. A Steering Council made up of religious leaders was subsequently formed to direct the process. Its first task was to guide the creation of other structural elements. In addition, the Ufungamano I Plenary highlighted the preparations that should be made by the assembled clergy, activists, and opposition politicians.The framework suggested that the Ufungamano Initiative should adopt a very loose structure as this would allow constituent groups to continue their own activities and feed into the larger movement. This suggests bureaucratic centralisation of some of the movement’s activities and structure, which is characteristic of an alliance or coalition, rather than a unitary movement. The agreed structure made no conscious effort to mobilise external institutions identified as stakeholders under the 1997 Review Act. It is instructive to note that the process of negotiating these structures revealed further cleavages between those who advocated greater citizen participation and therefore a grassroots presence and those who saw no need to establish an elaborate structure for this purpose.As the Ufungamano Initiative illustrates, a movement’s mobilising structures tend to reflect a complex mix of actors and the relationships between them. Hence, the Ufungamano Initiative’s organs came to reflect not only divergent positions within the movement but also significant distrust between some of the players. There were far too many competing interests of individuals, groups, regions, ethnicities, religions, and institutions that the emergent leadership structure had to accommodate. Consequently, the heterogeneity of actors with different competencies and agendas, as well as the multiplicity of leadership roles required by the movement, came to be reflected in the type of structure that evolved.^(46) Specifically, the structure reflected some agreement to disperse leadership roles across various nodes and to different stakeholders. Furthermore, the ensuing mobilisation structure was sufficiently flexible to allow ideological, social, and political pluralism to flourish by giving each group an opportunity to stress its particular interests without feeling “lost in the crowd” (Tarrow 1998 [1994]: 134). This suggests that leadership within the movement was largely predetermined by existing political and organisational structures. Indeed, the leaders of the various constituent groups shaped the organisational structure in accordance with their own backgrounds and previous experience, which in turn influenced the mobilisation, strategies, and outcomes of the movement. The final structure is illustrated in [Figure 4.1](#chapter04.xhtml_fig4_1).![Image](Images/2.jpeg)

Figure  4.1  The structure of the Ufungamano InitiativeNote: The conveners, as the religious leaders were known, formed the Steering Council. However, there were multiple leadership roles.Source: Minutes of Ufungamano Initiative Stakeholders’ Council meeting, January 19, 2000

### Stakeholders’ Forum/Plenary

The Stakeholders’ Forum/Plenary stood at the top of the organisational pyramid. It was the ultimate policy and decision-making organ and met formally at least once every three months. It was an open forum in which everybody who subscribed to the movement’s goals could congregate. This open-door policy was a strategic move aimed at ensuring that all reform-minded individuals and groups from a wide range of social, cultural, economic, and political backgrounds were welcomed to the initiative. It made decisions collegially: issues were brought to the forum, discussed, and usually agreed through consensus. Intractable disagreements were settled by votes, as happened with the proposal to merge with the parliamentary-led process (discussed in [Chapter 5](#chapter05.xhtml)).At its formation, the Stakeholders’ Forum comprised at least 54 formal representations from a wide range of actors, including subaltern movements, religious institutions, NGOs, youth movements, women’s organisations, and some opposition political parties that were identified as *bona fide* stakeholders in the 1997 Review Act. In the end, it brought together everyone who identified with the movement. The task of convening each Stakeholders’ Forum fell to the Steering Council.

### Steering Council

The idea of forming a Steering Council was first mooted during the Ufungamano I Plenary. Specifically, John Michuki, a prominent Democratic Party (official opposition) MP, proposed that two structures should be formed. One was a civic education committee whose role would be to identify methods for reaching out to the masses. The other was a council to offer leadership in the establishment of the organisational structure of a constitutional review commission. Michuki further proposed the use of existing structures and institutions, such as mosques, churches, and temples in addition to pro-change civil society organisations for the purpose of public mobilisation. He also argued for the need to identify other institutions with which the Ufungamano Initiative might collaborate at the grassroots level.Following these deliberations, a Steering Council was established to direct the movement’s development. Hence, it was the natural successor to the RCCT, which had initiated the discussions that culminated in the Ufungamano I Plenary. The plenary agreed that the Steering Council would be composed of 36 representatives drawn from religious groups that had been named in the 1997 Review Act and the 1998 Constitution of Kenya Review Commission Amendment Act as the key religious stakeholders in the constitutional reform process. Its principal role was to offer collegiate leadership. In its formative stages, this allowed for wide-ranging consultations that helped the Ufungamano Initiative to achieve consensus. In effect, the Steering Council was the movement’s cabinet, responsible for guiding and directing policy and meeting the aspirations of the Kenyan people through the review process.^(47) In order to determine those aspirations, it appointed task forces to investigate specific issues, discussed the resulting reports, and either approved or modified their proposals. As such, the Steering Council was responsible for setting up other organs. It also oversaw the appointment of PCK commissioners.Of significance here is the fact that various other contenders for the leadership of Ufungamano Initiative ceded stewardship of the movement to the clergy. This was a tacit compromise between sections of secular civil society led by the NCEC, which had been pushing for radical reform of the state for over a decade, and the opposition political elites, who always acted according to the exigencies of the moment. This seems to confirm Gusfield’s (1966: 137) suggestion that a movement’s formalisation into stable structures is invariably accompanied by a “decline of enthusiastic, agitational leadership” as these leaders are replaced by “statesmanlike, bureaucratic administrators.” The point here is that the emergence of an accommodating, dialogical leadership should be read in the context of the previous “failure” of more confrontational leaders within the NCEC to make any progress on constitutional reform. This suggests that, despite their social and political conservatism, a united religious leadership was still trusted to play a constructive role in uniting diverse pro-reform forces in a politically fragile and deeply divided society. Indeed, one of the chief functions of the Steering Council was to resolve disputes that arose in the course of the review process. This suggests that the movement’s leaders were well aware that the process would be arduous.As the Steering Council was responsible for overall leadership, it was also tasked with receiving the commission’s final report, disseminating this throughout Kenya, and finally organising a National Constitutional Conference – and later a referendum – to enable all Kenyans to participate in the new constitution’s ratification. These activities would demand huge financial and human resources that the Steering Council was responsible for gathering and deploying. However, raising funds proved difficult because most of the Western donors who had underwritten previous pro-change struggles were uneasy that the Ufungamano Initiative’s agenda might lead to radical transformations that would threaten their interests in Kenya. Hence, only a few (mainly Scandinavian) countries agreed to fund the new movement’s activities. As it turned out, though, stakeholders with significant traditional indigenous support, such as the NCCK and the Catholics, proved to be effective fundraisers. As such, they bore much of the financial burden of the review process.^(48) This resulted, as we will see shortly, in the leaders of these constituencies assuming more prominent roles in the leadership of the Steering Council and the movement more generally.Regardless of the movement’s limited funds, however, it was driven forward by ordinary people’s commitment to the cause and the volunteer spirit among its members. Thousands of people made “personal contributions and volunteered their time and services to support various efforts. Every Sunday, people contributed *sadaqah* [offerings].”^(49) Understood within the context of Kenya’s parlous economic state at the time, this was a truly great commitment. Individual members or constituent groups routinely offered spaces for meetings, food, accommodation, and, of course, their own time – all for free. These gifts complemented the financial donations made by a few supportive donors who constituted themselves into what they called the Friends of Ufungamano.One PCK commissioner suggested that most of the credit for dealing with donors should go to Mutava Musyimi, “a very brilliant person who will sit and think through an issue and ask, ‘What will this do?’”^(50) But this had the effect of giving the NCCK – and specifically its secretary general, Musyimi – great leverage in decision-making. The net result thus “turned out to be not as equalising as it ought to have been … because he who pays the piper, chooses the tune.”^(51) The dominance of the NCCK and its leader was exacerbated by the failure of the envisaged collegiate leadership to function effectively, as Musyimi soon established himself as “first among equals.” This and the legitimacy he came to enjoy as the movement’s *de facto* leader were attributable to his personal charisma, a structural vacuum within the organisation, and the financial resources and connections he and the NCCK commanded.

### The People’s Commission of Kenya (PCK)

The PCK was a technical organ with a constituency-based membership. Its composition was inherited from an earlier RCCT proposal regarding the number of members allocated to each constituency.^(52) As such, the names of most of those who took up positions in the PCK had already been submitted to parliament in line with the provisions of the 1997 Review Act and the 1998 Constitution of Kenya Review Commission Amendment Act before KANU and the NDP scuttled the review process. Of course, these two parties failed to take up their allocated positions in the PCK because they had already committed to the rival parliament-led process, so their slots were distributed among new candidates who represented different constituencies. Nevertheless, the fact that most of the commissioners had been nominated under the previous Act lends credence to the notion that the Ufungamano Initiative was determined to uphold the 1997 Review Act.Structurally, the PCK was subordinate and answerable to the Stakeholders’ Forum and the Steering Council. It was specifically tasked with working with the Civic Education Technical Committee to ensure coordinated civic education and access to all parts of the country. It also requested, received, collated, and analysed submissions from the public in order to table issues for debate and negotiation by the stakeholders with the ultimate aim of preparing a draft constitution.

### The Secretariat

The Steering Council was responsible for overseeing the operations of a small (14-member) Secretariat that dealt with the day-to-day administration of the movement and provided support for the Steering Council and the PCK. Three secretaries drawn from the Catholics, Protestants, and Muslims served as co-heads of the Secretariat, further entrenching the religious institutions’ control of the movement. Meanwhile, seven specialists covered areas such as constitutional matters, communications, logistics, finance, and administration.

### Specialised technical committees

Specialised technical committees were established to deal with a number of key areas. Nothing was fixed in terms of representation and distribution of positions on these committees. Rather, members were appointed according to their expertise. The most important committees were:

1.  Finance: Tasked with assisting the Steering Council in mobilising the movement’s financial resources and accountability for the same.
2.  Communications and Public Relations: Tasked with designing an effective communication strategy to provide speedy, accurate responses to questions or issues raised by the public about the review process and explain the initiative’s position on key issues. Also responsible for advising the movement generally and the Steering Council in particular on the suitability of promotional material, press statements and releases, and other written communications.
3.  Constitutional and Legal Affairs: The movement’s legal think-tank, this committee offered advice on how to adhere to the consensus achieved during the Bomas and Safari Park negotiations, essential principles of which were contained in the 1997 Review Act. Also provided alternative analysis of the law that was instrumental in information politics.
4.  Civic Education: Tasked with reaching out to Kenyans to rally them behind the review process, this committee worked closely with the PCK. Facilitated nationwide awareness raising in a coordinated and collaborative manner and ensured ordinary Kenyans’ meaningful, effective, and efficient participation in the review process.
5.  Public Mobilisations: Tasked with mobilising public support for the Ufungamano Initiative’s review process. Responsible for logistical preparations for public events, such as meetings, rallies, and tours of both the Steering Council and the PCK. Also assisted the Steering Council in developing a strategy for coordinating the participation of various communities in the review process.
6.  Parliamentary and Political Affairs: Tasked with mobilising political actors to support the review process, this committee worked closely with the Legal and Constitutional Affairs Committee to carry out regular reviews of the political environment and advised the Steering Council on evolving political trends and their potential impact on the review process. Also liaised with political parties to ensure effective communication and representation of the Ufungamano Initiative’s position in political debates.

All of these committees worked under the direction of the Steering Council in a well-oiled organisational machine. But what was the nature of the relationships between the various structural elements? Did the overall structure ensure sufficient participation and accountability? How did the movement ensure accountability among its component parts?The utilisation of the infrastructure offered by religious institutions certainly helped the movement to rival the state’s alternative process and reach all corners of Kenya. Arguably, without these structures, the Ufungamano Initiative would not have become as successful as it did. Indeed, all of its predecessors, and especially the NCEC, failed to achieve as much as the Ufungamano Initiative. However, the movement had few avenues for direct citizen participation, especially in the Stakeholders’ Forum. Although citizens were always free to attend the Forum and air their views, the formal designation of some organisations as “stakeholders” meant that many others were effectively excluded. Furthermore, most of the people who occupied positions in these structures were middle class. As such, there was a disconnect between the rhetoric of a people-led constitutional reform process and the internal organisation of the movement. This emanated from the absence of an enabling structure for direct citizen participation in the Stakeholders’ Forum. Therefore, despite styling itself as a popular movement, and although it championed ordinary people’s struggles, the Ufungamano Initiative was dominated by members of Kenya’s elite and middle class.It is paradoxical that Ufungamano Initiative enjoyed considerable popular support yet failed to create the structures that would have enabled it to reach out to all Kenyans. Instead, the movement’s structures were utilised to gain control over subalterns. Ultimately, its key leaders claimed to represent the views of ordinary Kenyans in the constitutional reform process even though they had no clear mandate to do so. The narrow interpretation of a people-led process that was the inevitable consequence of utilising the religious institutions’ existing infrastructure was fiercely contested by Muungano wa Mageuzi, which demanded an authentic people’s movement. Although unsuccessful, Muungano wa Mageuzi’s stance highlights tensions between representative claim-making and full participation of those affected. Such tensions were especially evident during the merger negotiations with the state-led process (see [Chapter 5](#chapter05.xhtml)).The movement countered accusations of elitism by insisting that issues of representation were contested, given questionable electoral practices. During the Ufungamano I Plenary, Matiba captured this well when he stated, “since 1983, elections have never been free in Kenya and therefore people do not trust most parliamentarians.” Another key player added, “the question of representation presupposes that elections are in themselves democratic.”^(53) Moreover, given the state’s hostility, the Ufungamano Initiative probably would have found it very difficult to secure a popular mandate from the people of Kenya.Notwithstanding its presence throughout the country (which the movement hailed as an irrefutable sign of its legitimacy), the Ufungamano Initiative’s arrogation of representation actually helped to undermine that legitimacy. While it presented itself as a popular movement, and to some extent supported that claim through the activities of the PCK, it is doubtful that its main aim was ever to ensure that Kenya’s lower orders played a significant role in drafting a new constitution.^(54)This is in no way to discount the fact that the Ufungamano Initiative’s position resonated with many Kenyans. Rather, the aim is to highlight the operational challenges the movement faced when trying to facilitate popular participation in its key decision-making structures. Despite its flaws and shortcomings, the Ufungamano Initiative did transparently outline the principles of its operations and engagement with the public, such as when declaring: “the process of the constitutional review in Kenya is as important as the eventual content of the constitution and the integrity of the process is central to the legitimacy of the [resultant] constitution.”^(55) Moreover, it adopted the principles of openness and transparency, inclusivity, accountability and cumulativeness, and self-correction as key aspects of constitution making as well as inalienable rights of all Kenyans. This was the Ufungamano Initiative’s enduring contribution to the constitutional reform project, notwithstanding the fact that its own fidelity to both the letter and spirit of these principles occasionally wavered in its operations and structures.

## Conclusion

The Ufungamano Initiative achieved a great deal through a combination of strong leadership, efficient structures, and effective strategies. For instance, it captured the public’s imagination, mobilised the citizenry, facilitated civic education, and started the process of collecting the public’s views on a new constitution. It also faced numerous challenges, not least how to raise funds and recruit sufficient personnel to undertake its mission. Nevertheless, against the odds, it generated such uncertainty in the Moi/KANU regime’s handling of the constitutional reform process that the regime ultimately started looking for a way out of the stalemate it had engineered. This was just one element in a credibility crisis that was rocking the government at the time.Even so, there was uncertainty over whether the Ufungamano Initiative would be able to press home its advantage. However, an unexpected opportunity suddenly arrived when the desperate Moi regime asked the renowned constitutional law scholar Yash Pal Ghai to steer its review process. Ghai agreed, but only on condition that the leaders of the Ufungamano Initiative were invited to participate in a common process. [Chapter 5](#chapter05.xhtml) explores how he convinced them to accept this invitation.As this chapter has shown, the key to the Ufungamano Initiative’s success was the way in which it framed constitution making as crucial to the lives of all Kenyans and therefore too important to be left to politicians alone. As such, the movement made the citizen central to the review process by arguing that the people were sovereign and had an inalienable right to choose the type of constitution they wanted. Second, it framed the constitution as a sacred covenant between the people and their rulers that required contributions from both. Hence, it fashioned itself as an effort to push for reform and, more fundamentally, to reclaim and rewrite the Kenyan people’s relationship with their political leaders in a more participatory way. However, its structures fell short of realising these goals. The Ufungamano Initiative opted for a simple but nested structure that relied on its constituent organisations’ existing infrastructures to mobilise resources and support. Although logistically effective, this exposed ever more cleavages in a movement that was already riddled with contradictions.Notes1    Kibwana, author interview, October 21, 2009.2    Minutes of RCCT meeting, December 3, 1999.3    Lethome, author interview, October 2, 2009.4    Ibid.5    Wandati, author interview, September 17, 2009.6    Indeed, countries such as Uganda, Eritrea, Ethiopia, and South Africa underwent constitutional reforms, while others such as Malawi, Tanzania, Zambia, DR Congo, Benin, Burundi, Congo, Namibia, and Nigeria embraced political pluralism, sometimes with substantial amendments to their constitutions (Cottrell and Ghai 2007; Whitaker and Giersch 2009). Mutua (2008: 141) notes that “the early 1990s saw many autocratic governments on the African continent ousted in a fever of regime change.” This fate befell Zambia, Mali, Benin, and Congo, among others.
