# [8    A turning point when history failed to turn?](#toc.xhtml_ch-08)

## Introduction

Although it resulted from decades of contentions with multiple dimensions, the 2010 constitution was, in essence, an elite bargain that was concluded to address a violent crisis (see [Chapter 7](#chapter07.xhtml)). Among others, North et al. (2013) argue that constitutions and other institutions are key to reducing the threat of violence because they have the capacity to shape relations between elites. However, they remind us that not all elite bargains have this effect. On the contrary, only those that incentivise elites to cooperate and coordinate rent generation reduce the threat of violence. Furthermore, such incentives are effective only when rents are liable to decline in the event of violence; and they are reinforced only when the leaders of competing groups accept existing enforcement institutions. The critical question, then, is: given the elite-bargain nature of the 2010 constitution, has it provided sufficient incentives for ongoing intra-elite cooperation?The mass of Kenyans who voted for the proposed constitution in the 2010 referendum saw it as an opportunity to reorder the relationship between themselves and Kenya’s power elites while also safeguarding the rule of law. Additionally, the document was sold to Kenyans as the best means to democratise socioeconomic opportunities, which, in turn, would lead to fairer resource distribution and reduce the risk of violence and social conflict. The broader question, then, is: how has the new constitution succeeded in embedding this vision of progressive reform? Specifically, which new institutional arrangements, incentives, and opportunities has the constitution introduced to deter violence, encourage cooperation between elites, and avert internecine violence? Furthermore, given the elites’ long history of disregard for the rule of law, how well are they adhering to the dictates of the 2010 constitution? And, crucially, has the constitution expanded opportunities for socioeconomic progress and cooperation for ordinary Kenyans?These questions are pertinent given that intra-elite electoral competition was the harbinger of the constitutional reform movement that crystallised in the early 1990s. Besides fuelling and sustaining this struggle through the periodic alignment of elite factions with popular forces, the intra-elite struggle for power, as indicated in [Chapter 7](#chapter07.xhtml), also resulted in a political order similar to that which Arias and Goldstein (2010)characterise as “violent pluralism,” especially after the reintroduction of multi-party politics in 1991\. The impact of the institutional changes wrought by the new constitution may be assessed through detailed analysis of the conduct and outcomes of the last two Kenyan general elections (2013 and 2017).As we saw in [Chapter 7](#chapter07.xhtml), Kenya was on the verge of a full-scale violent implosion following a dispute over the outcome of the 2007 presidential election in the context of a polity pockmarked by ethnic-based political mobilisation. Just over five years later, on March 4, 2013, Kenyans voted in another high-stakes, ethnically charged poll. As in 2007, there were disagreements over the presidential vote tally. However, on this occasion, relative peace prevailed due to a complex web of structural and contingent factors. Specifically, institutional reforms following the 2007/2008 post-election violence facilitated the dispersal of rent-seeking opportunities to a wider group of people. Meanwhile, further reforms increased public confidence in the autonomy of state institutions, especially the Supreme Court and the Electoral Commission. Consequently, in 2013, Raila Odinga – who was once again the ‘losing’ candidate in the presidential election – contested the outcome in the Supreme Court, rather than on the streets. Moreover, when the court ruled against him, although neither he nor civil society accepted the decision, Kenyans chose “peace over democracy” (Long et al. 2013) and decided to move on. Other factors that contributed to the peace dividend were contingent upon shifting subaltern attitudes and the circumstances (including interventions by other state institutions, local civil society, the media, and the International Criminal Court) under which the 2013 election was held. The international community, civil society, and the media all had roles to play in persuading Kenya’s competing elites to opt for peace rather than violence.However, the opposition’s acquiescence was grudging, at best, as it never accepted the outcome of either the election or the court case, which severely undermined public trust in both the judiciary and the Independent Electoral and Boundaries Commission (IEBC) as “neutral arbiters” of the intra-elite competition for power. As a result, the period between the 2013 and 2017 elections was marked by intense mobilisations against the IEBC, which was held accountable for mishandling the previous election.¹ Additionally, these inter-election years saw a failed push for another constitutional referendum, and thereafter street protests that forced the IEBC Commissioners to resign *en masse*. Consequently, a whole new group of commissioners had to be appointed only a few months before the 2017 election. The Supreme Court also continued to receive widespread criticism. This may have prompted the early retirement of the chief justice, which paved the way for a reconstituted court prior to the 2017 poll. Following the election, in a majority verdict, this new Supreme Court nullified the presidential election and ordered a fresh vote due to “illegalities and irregularities.” Odinga, however, withdrew from the rerun, triggering a widespread boycott across his support base. This raises three critical questions. What was different in 2017? What was the effect of the Supreme Court’s decision? And does the nullification of the election of an incumbent president suggest judicial independence and therefore institutional maturity?The next section outlines the context within which the 2013 election was held. Thereafter, the chapter presents and analyses the factors that contributed to relative peace in that year. It then turns to developments informed by the outcome of the 2013 election and explains how these changed the context and conduct of the 2017 election. This analysis reveals that inter-agency coordination in 2013 averted a crisis through the careful crafting of consent and a sense of “fairness.” However, by 2017, there was a sense that the institutions tasked with refereeing and arbitrating the intra-elite competition for power were no longer “fair.” The result was an escalation of protests and violence. Although the Supreme Court’s unexpected decision in favour of the opposition boosted the demonstrators’ legitimacy, it simultaneously eroded trust in the court among the ruling Jubilee Alliance’s supporters (Cheeseman et al. 2019). Odinga’s last-minute withdrawal from the rerun then complicated the situation, prompting further violent protests. Kenyatta and Odinga succeeded in cooling the political temperature with a public handshake, but this gesture also had the effect of fracturing the unity of the Jubilee Alliance. The chapter concludes that the fundamental defects in Kenya’s governance structures remain, not least because the institutions embedded in the 2010 constitution have proved incapable of constraining elite behaviour.

## The 2013 election in context

The 2010 constitution instituted a couple of reforms that dispersed power from the executive, especially through its introduction of checks and balances on the presidency. For example, the president could no longer make unilateral appointments; instead, his nominees were subject to parliamentary review and approval (Cheeseman et al. 2019). Additionally, the new constitution required the president to secure a popular mandate by garnering at least 50 per cent plus one of all votes cast. Given the ethnic-based nature of political mobilisations in Kenya (Mati 2019), this meant that anyone seeking the presidency was obliged to form a coalition. Anticipating that such coalitions would be inherently unstable, the constitution decreed that they must be deposited with the Registrar of Political Parties and run throughout the entire term. This has resulted in both opportunities and certainty for the new generation of elite electoral coalitions. Moreover, the constitution reinforces that certainty because the president can only dismiss his/her deputy via a parliamentary impeachment process.The 2010 constitution also reconfigured the organisation and exercise of political power by establishing several institutions that enabled a wider range of elite actors to benefit from rent-seeking and -distribution activities. Specifically, it created a two-chamber legislature with 349 MPs (up from 222 in the previous constitution) and a devolved system of governance that transferred power and resources to 47 counties. Fundamentally, in response to grievances relating to the country’s uneven development and unequal resource sharing, under Article 174, the constitution spells out that devolution will ensure equity in the distribution of national and local resources, with at least 15 per cent of the national budget allocated directly to county governments. The devolved units are tasked with giving “powers of self-governance to the people and enhanc[ing] the participation of the people in the exercise of the powers of the State and in making decisions affecting them.”² To this effect, each county elects a governor and a deputy, who in turn appoint an executive committee to deliver services to the community. Further, each county has an assembly of elected members who legislate local laws and act as a check on the local executive.The net effect of this devolution in the last two elections has been that a large number of politicians have opted to stand for the governorship of their local area, rather than the national parliament, as they know that this will give them access to substantial economic resources and local patronage networks. Additionally, devolution has resulted in no single coalition gaining control over every tier of power across the 47 counties. In 2013, for example, 23 of the 47 elected governors were affiliated to the Coalition for Reforms and Democracy. This meant that, under the new constitutional arrangements, they could help themselves to a sizeable share of the spoils even though their coalition lost the presidential vote. Cheeseman et al. (2019) label this phenomenon “national losers but local winners.” Of course, it is in the interests of these local winners to discourage destructive violence in their counties.The 2010 constitution also introduced checks and balances that gave independent state institutions – including the Registrar of Political Parties, the IEBC, the Political Parties Disputes Tribunal, the Ethics and Anti-Corruption Commission (EACC), the National Cohesion and Integration Commission (NCIC), and the judiciary – the necessary insulation from executive interference as well as some teeth to police the political elite’s behaviour. The judiciary, for example, was revamped through the establishment of a Supreme Court and the appointment of a new chief justice, Willy Mutunga, in 2011.³ Mutunga, a former political detainee, had made his name as an activist in the pro-reform movement since the 1980s. Many observers had faith in him to initiate some much-needed reform of the judiciary. Soon after his appointment, the judiciary underwent a systematic, transparent, and participatory vetting of its officers as per the transition clauses of the new constitution. This instilled some confidence among political players and their supporters that the judiciary would command respect as a trusted arbiter in future electoral disputes.The National Accord of February 2008 had decreed that the power to reconstitute and resource the Electoral Commission would be shared between President Kibaki, Prime Minister Odinga, and parliament. As a result, the discredited commissioners who had overseen the 2007 election were dismissed, and an interim Electoral Commission was established to oversee the 2010 referendum. Thereafter, under the transitional clauses of the new constitution, responsibility for appointing and resourcing the IEBC continued to be shared by Kibaki, Odinga, and parliament. This shared responsibility generated a degree of optimism that the revamped IEBC, unlike its predecessor, would oversee free and fair elections in future.As we shall see in the next section, these changes, coupled with important interventions from civil society, the media, and some other state institutions, helped to effect a relatively peaceful election in 2013.

## The forced choice for peace in the 2013 election

Institutions’ efficacy in preventing violence rests on the conditions within which they function and the choices made by competing social forces. Specifically, effective institutions, as Przeworski (2004: 528) argues, “prevent people from doing what they would have otherwise done or induce them to do what they otherwise would not have done.” But Przeworski also poses an interesting question: “how can we determine what people would or would not have done, if the institutions were not there?” This is a pertinent question for this chapter, as it encourages reflective analysis of how the new institutional arrangements influenced the behaviours and choices of leaders of the dominant elite coalitions and their supporters in the 2013 and 2017 elections.We have just seen that several independent institutions were established under the terms of the 2010 constitution. In the following subsections, we explore how these institutions and others encouraged key players to choose peace and thereby helped to avert fresh outbreaks of violence in 2013 and 2017\. Specifically, we analyse the roles of the International Criminal Court, state institutions (including the Supreme Court, the police, and constitutional commissions), civil society, and the media as well increasing subaltern consciousness in constraining the political elites’ behaviour.

### The International Criminal Court

As we saw in [Chapter 7](#chapter07.xhtml), one of the measures taken to help the nation recover from the trauma of the 2007/2008 crisis was the establishment of a Commission of Inquiry into the Post-Election Violence (CIPEV), which was chaired by Judge Phillip Waki of the Court of Appeal. CIPEV held a series of public hearings and released its final report on October 16, 2008\. This made a number of recommendations, including reform of the police and the constitution, and the establishment of a special tribunal to investigate and prosecute the perpetrators of the violence. Failing this, it advised that the matter could be referred to the International Criminal Court (ICC) (CIPEV 2008; ICTJ 2014). To this effect, CIPEV submitted a sealed envelope containing the names of those whom it suspected of bearing the greatest responsibility for the violence, along with six boxes of evidence, to the Panel of Eminent Africans for initial safe keeping and subsequent conveyance to either the special tribunal or the ICC prosecutor.Although Kibaki and Odinga urged their lieutenants to introduce a bill to establish the tribunal, political brinkmanship resulted in the proposal’s defeat in parliament. Consequently, on July 9, 2009, Annan’s Panel of Eminent Africans announced that it had submitted the sealed envelope and the boxes of evidence to the ICC prosecutor. Thereafter, the prosecutor launched investigations that culminated in the indictment of six Kenyans: former Police Commissioner Major General Hussein Ali, Deputy Prime Minister Uhuru Kenyatta, head of the civil service Francis Muthaura, Higher Education Minister William Ruto, Henry Kosgey MP, and radio journalist Joshua arap Sang. By 2013, the ICC had confirmed charges against three of the six: Ruto, who was charged with mobilising and funding Kalenjin youth to attack Kikuyu in Eldoret; Kenyatta, who was charged with mobilising and funding Mungiki retaliatory attacks against perceived ODM supporters, especially in Nakuru and Naivasha; and journalist Joshua arap Sang for his alleged role in spreading hate through his popular Kalenjin vernacular breakfast show.Once indicted, Kenyatta (a Kikuyu) and Ruto (a Kalenjin) and their allies created a narrative of victimisation of their people in the post-election violence and held Odinga personally responsible for the ICC’s intervention (Capital FM 2012). This served to unite the two ethnic groups in a coalition of convenience – christened the Jubilee Alliance – just before the 2013 election (Mamdani 2013). Around the same time, Odinga, Vice-President Kalonzo Musyoka, and Minister for Trade Moses Wetangula formed the rival Luo–Kamba Coalition for Reforms and Democracy (CORD), which also enjoyed substantial support among coastal Kenyans.As the elections approached, the ICC’s eyes remained firmly trained on Kenya and a number of increasingly fearful actors within the dominant coalitions.⁴ Additionally, several Western diplomats voiced their opposition to the Jubilee Alliance, with the most notable intervention coming from US Undersecretary of State Johnnie Carson, who counselled, “choices have consequences” (Brown and Raddatz 2014; Mueller 2014). However, this merely served to reinforce the Kikuyu–Kalenjin alliance (and indeed the rival CORD coalition).Given the intrigues of ethnic-based political mobilisations, political scientist Mutahi Ngunyi predicted that the presidential election would be decided by a “tyranny of numbers,”⁵ because the Jubilee Alliance was sure to mobilise sufficient supporters among its constituent ethnic groups to defeat the Luo–Kamba coalition in the first round. His analysis was widely criticised at the time, primarily because it contradicted a host of opinion polls that forecast no candidate would achieve the threshold of 50 per cent plus one vote that would negate the need for a second round. Moreover, many CORD-allied intellectuals and a band of so-called “progressive forces” argued that Kenyans would not make “irrational choices” by electing a president who had just been indicted by the ICC. Maina (2013), for example, insisted that Ngunyi’s predictions were flawed and his conclusions not just implausible but plainly mistaken and probably propagandistic.In the end, Ngunyi was proved correct (see also Bratton and Kimenyi 2008 for analysis of similar voting patterns in the previous election). Nonetheless, among others, Mamdani (2013), Burbidge (2014), Carrier and Kochore (2014), Ferree *et al*. (2014), Willis and Chome (2014), Lynch (2014), Moss and O’Hare (2014), Cheeseman *et al*. (2014) question the ethnic thesis, especially in the 2013 election, arguing instead that it was an “issue-based contest.” For example, given widespread cynicism about the political elites, Burbidge (2014) and Lynch (2014) suggest that communities either simply support the most persuasive coalition or come under pressure from their co-ethnics to keep certain feared “others” out of office and vote for their “own” candidates instead. This is framed as false consciousness among the poor, who continue to idolise the leaders who marginalise them.For Mamdani (2013), Ngunyi’s conclusion “fails to recognize the issues that drive the ethnicization of politics.” For example, he argues that the land question was instrumental in CORD’s success in the coastal regions, where the land issue was “defined as a contest between the Kenyatta family and the people.” However, CORD failed in another area with a similar land problem – the Rift Valley – because “The Kalenjin followed Ruto who told them they had been sacrificed as lambs at the alter called ICC … Instead of a repeat of the 2007 ethnic conflict, you had an ethnic reconciliation” (Mamdani 2013). That said, the ICC was not the only driver that forced hitherto belligerent groups to set aside their differences. The work of many local civil society groups in potentially volatile areas also helped to maintain the peace. Additionally, state institutions and the media urged people to reject violence.

### Civil society, state institutions, and the media

There were massive peace campaigns prior to, during, and after the 2013 election, especially in those parts of the country deemed to be hotspots for political violence. These campaigns, as well as widespread public support for peace, helped to delegitimise violence. For example, in Kisumu, both secular and religious civil society groups organised massive civic education programmes while the media preached a message of peace. Prior to the election, groups such as the Amani Peace Forum, the Grassroots Trust, the Catholic Church, and the NCCK’s Justice and Peace Commissions worked with the local police and the provincial administration to target, mobilise, and organise youth in Kisumu towards maintaining peace, irrespective of the outcome of the election. Similarly, in Mombasa, focus group discussions and interviews with residents of Maweni (Likoni) and Mnazi Moja (Nyali) informal settlements highlighted the effectiveness of peace campaigns and public education programmes organised by local community groups, churches, mosques, and individual leaders.These interventions combined sociological, political economy, theological, and psychological approaches such as community mobilisation and prayer vigils to delegitimise violence. In Kisumu, for instance, given the widespread assumption that residents – especially those living in informal settlements – would turn violent if Odinga lost the election, the intervention strategy revolved around educating citizens that violent protests would work against their favoured candidate and have a negative impact on the Luo community’s image and economic investment in the city.⁶ Violence, it was further argued, affected the urban poor more than the rich, as the 2007/2008 violence – which was concentrated in urban slums – had indicated.The majority of Kisumu’s slum dwellers are members of the precariat, subsisting on the margins of the formal economy. Young men in the informal settlements survive on the proceeds of *boda boda* (mainly motorcycle or bicycle taxi) businesses. They comprise a major sector of this society, and they are occasionally mobilised as footsoldiers in political violence, as was the case 2007/2008\. To avoid a similar crisis in 2013, prior to the election, *boda boda* operators and other inhabitants of Kisumu’s informal settlements were mobilised to form neighbourhood watch groups who preached peace and countered misinformation that was likely to incite violence. By April 2013, this umbrella organisation had over 30,000 members and its central committee comprised 20 individuals elected by each of the city’s zones. Working in tandem with other groups and the police, they ensured that peace was maintained throughout Kisumu.One of the groups founded by *boda boda* operators was the 21 Brothers Self-Help Group. According to their chairperson, violence remained a constant threat during the 2013 election, but they always managed to avert it:There was an incident at one of the local bars where people we did not even know tried to get us worked up, telling us that there was massive evidence of vote tampering and we needed to go out on the streets. We refused to take this and told local people to maintain peace. Moreover, we stressed that, no matter what, there will be a loser and a winner.⁷A female Muungano wa Wanavijiji leader in Kisumu’s Corner Mbutta informal settlement similarly recalled defusing several tense situations as well as calling on the police to cool hot tempers.⁸ Such cooperation between community groups and the police was in marked contrast to 2008, when most of the deaths in Kisumu (64 out of 81) had resulted from police gunshots (see [Chapter 7](#chapter07.xhtml)). In 2013, the formerly trigger-happy police were calm and conciliatory in seeking solutions before situations spiralled out of control.Many of the inter-agency interventions in Kisumu articulated political economy arguments that drew on narratives of socioeconomic losses suffered by community members during election-related violence since the reintroduction of multi-party democracy. For instance, one representative of a group of HIV-positive women argued that past violence, especially in 2007/2008, had forced community health workers and NGOs to leave or suspend their services due to personal safety considerations.⁹ Of course, such moves were highly detrimental to the health of HIV-positive residents who depended on anti-retroviral drugs.Meanwhile, the chairperson of the *boda boda* operators’ central committee highlighted the economic impact of violence:When violence breaks out, the cost of living goes up, and it is the common man [*sic*] who suffers. In 1992, for instance, the Kalenjin were attacked [by the Luo] in Sondu [a market place for traders from neighbouring Nandi and Kisumu districts]. They never came back after that. The once-thriving market is now long dead. In 2007/2008, a similar thing happened in Kisumu. When violence broke out, we did not have food supplies in the market for a long time … In 2007, politicians incited *boda boda* operators to chase away people of other ethnic groups. These are the same people who gave us business. So, we lost economic opportunities … Besides, a majority of those killed by the police were the poor *boda boda* operators; the high and the mighty were never touched. Why do we need to continue making our lives difficult and dying this way? We need a different way of doing politics and expressing dissent.^(10)This testimony suggests burgeoning political consciousness among ordinary Kenyans. Such an interpretation is reinforced by the fact that the Luo-Nyanza electorate defied Odinga and returned candidates from other parties in addition to ODM. Moreover, this sort of subaltern protest against a co-ethnic national political elite was not confined to Kisumu. Something similar occurred in Othaya, where voters defied Kibaki’s call to reject a controversial businesswoman, Mary Wambui, who ran against the outgoing president’s preferred candidate. This was highly significant given that Kibaki had represented Othaya since 1974. Meanwhile, in Nairobi, poorer voters overwhelmingly supported Mike Sonko – a flamboyant politician who presented himself as their champion – as their first senator. In addition, a number of Members of Parliament were elected in areas where their ethnic cohorts are marginal, such as Kenyan-Indians in Meru, Nairobi, and Kisumu, a Somali in Migori, and a Luo in Mombasa. This signalled a shift away from the ethnic-based politics that had prevailed in Kenya since the resumption of multi-party democracy. Moreover, it was not a one-off, as the pattern was repeated in various parts of the country in the 2017 election. For example, the Kenyan-Indian MPs from Meru and Kisumu retained their seats; an Indian-born doctor was elected in a rural constituency in Uasin Gishu County in the Rift Valley; and a Borana was elected in Nyali, Mombasa.So, how should we interpret voters’ sudden defiance of politicians who had previously commanded near-fanatical support in their heartlands? Did this behavioural shift signify increased subaltern awareness that, when united, they can dictate the direction of politics? Are the years of civil society-led civic education finally bearing fruit, albeit sometimes in contradictory ways? There is a growing belief that a rebellion against the nation’s political elites has begun, with subaltern voters now making decisions on the basis of whom they think will serve them best, especially at the local level. While it will take several elections to confirm this conclusion, journalists were quick to report that some of Odinga’s traditional supporters in Nyanza were unhappy with his family’s near-total political dominance at the local level (Otieno 2013).^(11) Similarly, three of former President Moi’s sons lost elections in their heartland – the Rift Valley. Writing about South Africa, Von Holdt (2014: 136) suggests that violent protests against traditional leaders communicate “anger and urgency to the authorities.” However, it is not just violence directed at political elites that signifies rebellion. In itself, the *absence* of mass violence in 2013 may be viewed as a symbolic rebellion because, as some Kisumu residents pointed out, Odinga’s traditional supporters were no longer willing to lay down their lives for him.^(12)

### Institutions and the behaviour of the political elites

Notwithstanding the previous discussion, we should not discount the impact of the political elites’ constant requests for peace or CORD’s decision to challenge the presidential election result in the Supreme Court with an undertaking that it would accept the verdict. Both of these factors reduced the likelihood of Odinga’s supporters resorting to violence. For example, in his address to the nation on March 9, after Kenyatta had been declared the winner, Odinga announced his absolute faith in the Supreme Court to deliver a judgement in his favour.^(13) CORD supporters remained calm because they shared his optimism that the Supreme Court would overturn the results announced by the IEBC, not least because Odinga claimed he had irrefutable evidence of vote tampering.Moreover, the leaders of the other political coalitions played important roles in placating their supporters and called on their competitors to do the same. In this regard, early concessions by Peter Kenneth and Musalia Mudavadi before the final vote tally had been announced exerted considerable pressure on Odinga to follow suit (or at least seek redress in the courts, rather than on the streets). As a result, although Odinga refused to concede defeat and accused the IEBC of bias, he urged his supporters to remain calm and lodged his petition with the Supreme Court (Capital FM 2013; *Daily Nation* 2013). Likewise, the Jubilee Alliance played within the rules by defending their victory in court.That said, there were signs of increasing tension after Odinga filed his petition. First, fractures emerged among ODM supporters in Nyanza amid accusations that some of Odinga’s traditional support bases had voted for other candidates. Second, the CORD leadership announced a series of rallies ostensibly to “thank electorates for their support.” Third, a public spat between Odinga and Kenyatta after the former claimed victory prompted a flood of responses from the national security establishment, the Supreme Court, and some members of the clergy (Beja and Oketch 2013; Marete 2013). The inspector general of police, the National Security Advisory Council (NSAC), and the Supreme Court all objected to CORD’s planned rallies due to fears that they would incite violence. The Supreme Court also warned politicians not to discuss the case, given that it was sub judice, and urged the media to report its proceedings impartially and fairly (Ogemba 2013). Meanwhile, the inspector general of police banned mass action, demonstrations, and political gatherings. Meeting the same day, NSAC^(14) upheld this directive and supported the Supreme Court’s pronouncement. The NCCK and the National Cohesion and Integration Commission (NCIC)^(15) then jumped on the bandwagon, urging Kenyans to remain peaceful while they awaited the Supreme Court’s decision. There were conspicuous similarities between the NCCK and NSAC statements, both of which singled out Odinga and Kenyatta and urged them “to show leadership … and to refrain from statements and actions that would provoke hatred and tensions” (Barasa 2013). The cumulative pressure of these interventions forced CORD into a hasty retreat as it announced the suspension of all political activity until the Supreme Court ruled on the petition (Fortunate 2013). This decision seemed to confirm the increased authority of certain state and civil society institutions to constrain political elite behaviour.

### The media

The Kenyan media also helped to secure the peace dividend by urging restraint and, in some instances, refusing to broadcast or print anything that might incite violence. On February 10 and 25, just a few weeks prior to the election, a number of media houses organised and moderated two presidential debates that gave ordinary Kenyans precious opportunities to engage with candidates on important public policy issues, such as land, foreign affairs, the economy, homeland security, and, most importantly, the ICC.^(16) In these debates, all of the candidates expressed their confidence in the IEBC to deliver a credible election, and in the judiciary to act as a neutral arbiter in the event of a dispute. They further committed to maintain the peace, accept the results, and not incite their supporters to violence should they lose. Furthermore, throughout the election campaign and on into the Supreme Court petition period, the nation’s media houses urged Kenyans to conduct themselves in a peaceful manner and send an unequivocal message to “hordes of foreign correspondents [who had] flocked to Kenya, many of them to report on how ‘primitive tribal Africans’ cannot manage a simple election” (Mutiga 2013).Under these circumstances, even after the Supreme Court ruled in favour of Kenyatta, Odinga kept his word and *respected* the verdict. However, neither he nor civil society *accepted* it, which severely undermined public confidence in the whole judiciary (and the IEBC). It is thus unclear whether the IEBC and the Supreme Court should be cast as “neutral arbiters” after the 2013 election. Arguments advanced by scholars such as Shah (2015) and Long et al. (2013) that Kenya’s citizens respected the results primarily because they wanted peace to prevail seem to be supported by national opinion polls, which suggest that many viewed the election as credible specifically because it was relatively peaceful. Unfortunately, though, as we will see shortly, 2013 might turn out to be no more than a brief, peaceful hiatus in Kenya’s turbulent electoral history unless faith in its state institutions is restored.

## The 2017 election in context

Odinga’s truculent reaction to the election result and the Supreme Court decision helped to discredit the judiciary, the IEBC, and other state institutions in the eyes of many voters and greatly increased the likelihood of violence in future elections. CORD continued to insist that a biased IEBC had robbed it of victory in 2013, while the Supreme Court was impotent. Mobilisations to change the constitution – and especially the IEBC – soon coalesced under the banner of a new movement: Okoa Kenya (Save Kenya).This organisation launched a peaceful campaign to collect the million signatures that were necessary to force a referendum on a new constitution and duly submitted a petition containing no fewer than 1.6 million signatures to the IEBC on November 9, 2015.^(17) The IEBC then embarked on its standard verification process. Several months went by with no word from the commission, which led a frustrated CORD leadership to organise a series of weekly sit-ins at the IEBC offices. Finally, on March 22, 2016, the IEBC declared that the petition had been rejected as it failed to meet the required constitutional threshold. This was because the commission’s verification process had established that only 891,598 of the 1.6 million signatures were valid (Simiyu 2016). CORD’s leaders were livid and launched a campaign of weekly protests and picketing that demanded reform of the IEBC prior to the forthcoming general election (*Daily Nation* 2016).By mid-2016, the IEBC’s position had become untenable. Hence, yet another elite bargain was needed to avert the looming crisis. On October 5, after a sustained campaign by Odinga and his supporters, the IEBC commissioners were forced to resign (*The Economist* 2016). A new commission was appointed in January 2017, seven months before the general election. Odinga expanded his coalition by uniting with Musalia Mudavadi’s Amani Coalition to form the National Super Alliance (NASA) shortly before the country went to the polls on August 8\. However, Odinga remained the principal flag bearer, with Kalonzo Musyoka of the Wiper Party installed as his deputy.The 2017 election was the first real test of the devolution arrangements and institutional changes that the 2010 constitution had initiated. Therefore, it was regrettable that it was held under the auspices of an electoral commission with only seven months’ experience. In addition, Cheeseman et al. (2019) and Kanyinga and Odote (2019) have suggested that the new commission seemed jinxed from the start, with opposition parties challenging its work in the courts throughout the electoral process. Then, on July 27, 2017, the murder of Christopher Msando – head of information, communication, and technology at the IEBC – heightened the animosity and mistrust between the two dominant political camps. The opposition parties hinted that the murder was part of a plot by the Jubilee Alliance to rig the election (Moseti and Mukinda 2017). Moreover, it came just days after an attacker killed a police officer outside the deputy president’s home in Eldoret. There were widespread fears that a repetition of the violence of 2007/2008 was becoming inevitable.Thankfully, the voting itself proceeded in an atmosphere of relative peace and calm. However, the situation deteriorated rapidly from the moment when the first results were announced. Kenyatta took an early lead but thereafter the gap between him and the other contenders remained almost constant. This prompted Odinga to issue a statement to the effect that the IEBC’s servers must have been hacked. He called for the counting to be suspended until his claims had been investigated, but the IEBC carried on regardless. On August 10, the commissioners announced a provisional result that gave Kenyatta 54.4 per cent of the vote against Odinga’s 44 per cent, even though they had not yet received the requisite scans of results forms from all of the nation’s polling stations. In addition, they failed to offer any explanation for the delay. Nevertheless, the following day, the IEBC officially declared Kenyatta the winner. Most of the international observers (from the African Union, the Carter Center, the Commonwealth of Nations, the East African Community, and the European Union) gave the election a provisional clean bill of health, even though several raised concerns over the unusually high number of spoilt ballots. However, NASA and Odinga refused to accept the result and claimed that the election had clearly been rigged in Kenyatta’s favour. Furthermore, NASA published its own figures that seemed to confirm an Odinga victory (Cheeseman *e*t al. 2019). This time, in contrast to 2013, Odinga announced that he would not put his case to the Supreme Court as he had lost all faith in the institution. Almost immediately, protests against “Odinga’s stolen votes broke out in various parts of the country. By the 10 of August, five protestors had already been killed by police” (Al Jazeera 2017).Two articles in a recent special issue of the *Journal of Eastern African Studies* (Cheeseman *et al*. 2019; Kanyinga and Odote 2019) offer detailed analysis of the events that took place in the immediate aftermath of the election. As such, suffice to say that, notwithstanding his earlier declaration that he had no faith in the instutition, Odinga did eventually seek redress in the Supreme Court, submitting his petition with the barbed rejoinder that it now had an opportunity to redeem itself. To the surprise of many, on September 1, 2017, in a majority (four to two) ruling, the court found that the presidential election had not been conducted in accordance with either the constitution or the applicable law. Consequently, it held that the results were invalid, null, and void, and insisted that a fresh election must be held within 60 days (*Daily Nation* 2017). This was a first for the whole continent of Africa. Although Kenyatta said he respected the decision, he was furious and publicly threatened to “revisit the judiciary,” labelling the judges *wakora* (thugs). Meanwhile, predictably, the opposition hailed the ruling as a triumph of justice.However, Odinga pulled out of the rerun, which was scheduled for October 26, on the grounds that the fundamental reforms ordered by the Supreme Court had not been implemented. One of the most important of these was reform of the IEBC (Aglionby 2017). On October 18, one of the commissioners, Roselyn Akombe, fled to the United States, claiming her life was in danger and the commission was under siege (BBC 2017). Nevertheless, despite this and the twin threats of a boycott and violent protests in Odinga’s strongholds, the remaining commissioners decided to press ahead with the election. A confrontation between NASA supporters and the state now seemed inevitable. The Supreme Court ruling and the ensuing IEBC crisis eroded much of the public’s trust in the commission, which “declined from a high of 74 per cent in July 2017 … to 40 per cent a few weeks short of the fresh presidential election” (Kanyinga 2019: 20).In the event, no polls were held in most of Odinga’s strongholds in Nyanza and coastal Kenya. Indeed, only 39 per cent of the electorate bothered to vote, mainly in Central Kenya and the Rift Valley, which allowed Kenyatta to win 98 per cent of the much-depleted ballot. According to Kanyinga (2019: 20), this turn of events dented the legimitacy of both Kenyatta (who could scarcely claim a popular mandate, given the low turnout) and Odinga (who was criticised for giving up too early). Inevitably, once again, NASA insisted that it would not recognise Kenyatta’s win. It also founded the National Resistance Movement (NRM), which organised boycotts of companies deemed close to Kenyatta, distributed uniforms to its members, and was at pains to display what Tilly (2004) calls “WUNC” (worthiness, unity, numbers, and commitment). The months that followed were tense, with police deployed to quell riots and demonstrations, especially in Nairobi, Mombasa, and Kisumu. The authorities killed more than 50 people – including a number of children – in these actions.On the early afternoon of January 30, 2018, the veteran leader of ODM, CORD, and NASA, Raila Odinga, was sworn in as the “People’s President of Kenya.” This brief but tense ceremony at Uhuru Park, Nairobi, marked the culmination of months of increasing hostility between NASA and the ruling Jubilee Alliance. A day later, the country’s internal security minister declared the NRM an illegal organisation. This was the signal for police officers to arrest the NRM’s self-declared “general,” Miguna Miguna, a controversial lawyer who had accepted Odinga’s pledge during the swearing-in ceremony.There was a precursor to the opposition leader’s mock-oath. Twenty years earlier, a more potent – but largely peaceful – movement, the Ufungamano Initiative, had sworn in its own commissioners to guide a participatory constitutional review process that would ultimately force the KANU–NDP regime to abandon its official process and seek a compromise (see [Chapter 5](#chapter05.xhtml)). In the stand-off between the state and the Ufungamano Initiative, Odinga, Mudavadi, Kalonzo, and Wetangula had all been members of the regime that had attempted to thwart a genuinely popular process in favour of their own exclusionary version. Now, 20 years later, they had adopted the very tactic that the Ufungamano Initiative had deployed so effectively against them. And, once again, it worked. Although Odinga’s mock-oath heightened tensions between Jubilee and NASA supporters, it brought Kenyatta to the negotiating table. Six weeks later, a series of secret talks culminated in the now infamous handshake between the two men on March 9, 2018\. Subsequently, Odinga was appointed the African Union’s special envoy for infrastructure.However, while the handshake cooled the political temperature between the country’s two main coalitions, it opened fractures within the ruling Jubilee Alliance. This was because some interpreted it as a flagrant attempt to prevent Deputy President William Ruto from succeeding Kenyatta at the end of the latter’s term of office. Yet, at the same time, the handshake encouraged a degree of bridge-building between Kenyatta and Odinga supporters with a view to overhauling the constitution and putting an end to election-related violence.Therefore, barely eight years after Kenya’s citizens voted overwhelmingly in favour of its current constitution, large sections of the country’s political elites are demanding root-and-branch constitutional reform in order to address a number of fundamental deficiencies in the governance architecture. As North et al. (2013) have argued, the existing document was the result of elite bargaining, so it was only ever going to paper over the problem of violence. The irony is that Kenya’s political elites, instead of investing in strong institutions and then affording them unqualified support, seem hell-bent on manufacturing another elite bargain. This begs the question: why has the struggle for a just and democratic electoral system been reduced to these backroom deals? One thing is clear, whatever the outcome of the latest elite bargain, it will not provide a long-term solution to the issue of electoral violence or Kenya’s governance woes.

## Conclusion

This chapter has reflected on the question of whether the 2010 constitution has cured the crises that ignited the constitutional reform struggle. Specifically, it has asked whether the constitution radically transformed Kenya’s governance institutions and practices with a view to expanding opportunities for ordinary citizens and, more importantly, created effective institutions for mediating and managing intra-elite conflicts to avoid repeated eruptions of violence. The elections of 2013 and 2017 serve as useful case studies when answering these questions because the distribution of resources among the elites and the masses – and especially the intra-elite competition for those resources in an ethnically mobilised political environment – has always lain at the heart of the Kenyan constitutional reform struggle and the nation’s long history of electoral violence.This analysis of elite behaviour and the roles of state institutions in the 2013 and 2017 elections reveals that the 2010 constitution failed to rectify some of the fundamental contradictions in the relationship between Kenya’s hegemonic elites and the country’s citizens. Reflecting on the performance of state institutions, especially in the context of the 2017 election, Cheeseman et al. (2019: 229) quote Helmke and Levitsky (2004) when arguing that “formal and informal institutions may compete, with the latter undermining the former, but customs and norms may also support strong democratic institutions and compensate for weak ones.” This means that, although the institutional reforms embedded in the 2010 constitution have dispersed rent-seeking opportunities among a somewhat wider political elite, which has ensured a greater degree of elite cooperation, Kenya is still a long way from achieving a just and democratic political order. This is because the elites need to be confident that state institutions are entirely neutral and unbiased when arbitrating their competition. Moreover, ethnic identity and patronage continue to be the master frame in political mobilisation, and predatory elite behaviour is far from contained.Notes1    “Raila Odinga: Democracy on trial”: [www.youtube.com/watch?v=zmF2rDbfb70](http://www.youtube.com).
