# [9    Conclusion](#toc.xhtml_ch-09)

## Introduction

For the past three decades, the Kenyan socioeconomic and political landscape has featured a constant struggle aimed at embedding a new constitutional order to check the excesses of the country’s political and economic elites. When a new constitution was finally promulgated on August 27, 2010, it was the culmination of a sustained two-decade struggle. This struggle resulted in the formation of alliances of disparate movements from the grassroots, sections of the middle class, and factions of Kenya’s political, economic, and religious elites to challenge the government. Most of the existing literature neglects the role of the everyday struggles of the masses in the constitutional reform project, privileging instead the role of middle-class professionals and their organisations. In addition, existing analyses of these struggles ignore the fact that there have been multiple waves of contention. This book casts its net wider, highlighting the roles of different classes in these contentions as well as the various waves of contention. This concluding chapter asks: how might these struggles inform contemporary scholarship of social movements and protest politics, especially in African contexts?The chapter has three main sections. The first looks at the socio-historical context within which these contentions evolved with a view to answering the question: what led to the emergence of the contemporary constitutional reform movement in Kenya? First, it is argued that contemporary struggles for state reform were products of both agency and structure in the domestic and international arenas. Specifically, both leadership and conscious action by citizens (i.e. agency) helped to change the conditions under which they found themselves. Second, it is argued that such agency was informed by existing socioeconomic and political conditions (i.e. structure).The second section revisits the waves of contention with a view to illuminating the various cycles of the constitutional reform struggle.This leads on to the final section, which focuses on the wave of contention that was dominated by the Ufungamano Initiative with a view to answering the question: how do disparate socioeconomic groups and competing interests cohere into a movement and subsequently force fundamental constitutional change. The movement’s contribution to the struggle for constitutional reform as well as its limitations will be explored in this section.

## Generative factors in contemporary socio-political protests in Kenya

As we have seen throughout this book, two core factors explain the emergence of the contemporary struggle for constitutional and political reform in Kenya. The first of these is agency, in the form of leadership and conscious action by citizens to change the conditions in which they found themselves. Closely related to agency is structure, in the form of the prevailing socioeconomic and political conditions in the country. Both of these generative factors are now explained and analysed in detail.

### Political and economic structure and the leadership nexus

Contemporary socio-political struggles in Kenya emerged in response to existing – and especially deteriorating – socioeconomic conditions and new political opportunities. Four closely related factors precipitated this new wave of contention. The first of these was an upheaval in the global political economy following the end of the Cold War, which prompted Western donors to shift their aid priorities towards democratisation and human rights. Specifically, the Paris Club of donors suspended aid to the Kenyan government pending democratisation of the political space and simultaneously channelled funds directly to civil society democracy campaigners (Maina 1998). One of the effects of this was that the Moi/KANU state became challengeable. However, the counterpoint to this was that civil society’s increasing reliance on foreign aid meant that a genuinely radical transformation of Kenyan society became less likely. There is a need to consider just how radical movements that benefit from foreign aid can be, especially when those who provide the aid stand to lose from radical transformations.Closely related to changing geopolitical priorities was the second factor – the fiscal austerity measures of the donors’ neoliberal Structural Adjustment Programmes (SAPs). These entailed reduced funding for the Moi/KANU state along with demands for less public expenditure on basic welfare services, the introduction of user fees, the privatisation of public enterprises and services, and a drastic reduction in the state wage bill. The reduced funding had numerous serious effects: the collapse of essential welfare services; the erosion of basic social welfare solidarity; rising inequality; and massive state labour layoffs that pushed many people into unemployment, with a majority ending up in slums. The resulting socioeconomic malaise bred widespread Polanyi-type responses against the Moi/KANU regime that were similar to those in other parts of Africa and around the globe. In this regard, Stiglitz (2002) records SAPs-motivated regime changes in Indonesia, Zambia, Malawi, Zaire, among other countries.Third, runaway corruption made matters significantly worse, especially for the poor. Specifically, as already noted, cuts in external funding decreased the economic resources that were available to the Moi/KANU regime to maintain its patronage network. Therefore, to forestall fragmentation of the dominant political elite coalition, Moi turned to his one remaining resource – land. Thereafter, his political cronies were allowed to grab public land while the state looked the other way. In urban slums especially, the elites used state security agencies and hired goons to evict the poor from their homes (Kanyinga and Njoka 2002; Klopp 2008; Macharia 1992). Such evictions had become commonplace by the mid-1990s (Wamucii and Idwasi 2011; Klopp 2008; Katumanga 2005; Katumanga with Cliffe 2005; Manundu 1997). As a result, Katumanga (2005: 510) concludes that, “for most urban lumpen elements, direct resistance was a function of the fact that they had no fall-back position and everything to lose” (see also Anderson 2002; Kobiah 1978). It was in this context that the working class, especially slum dwellers, started organising to protest against the evictions and other economic injustices. One such organisation was Muungano wa Wanavijiji (see [Chapter 3](#chapter03.xhtml)). Thereafter, these urban poor movements united with middle-class groups in a common struggle for constitutional reform, as they attributed their suffering to bad policies and believed that a new constitution would address their plight. In this regard, a key concern for the slum dwellers who participated in the constitutional reform struggle was land reform to enable them to gain titles to the areas they occupied.The endemic corruption did not spare middle-class professionals. For instance, lawyers – especially those who took on social justice cases – were increasingly harassed, abducted, or detained without trial. Moreover, the harassment extended to their families. In the words of one of the leading lights in this struggle, “there was no more law to practise” because the courts were controlled by the executive, leaving lawyers very vulnerable.¹ Under these circumstances, they chose to challenge the system in the hope that this would free them from political and economic oppression. Rational self-interest driven by economic necessity was therefore a critical factor in the burgeoning demand for reform among citizens who wanted to wrestle themselves out of the clutches of an avaricious state. This view challenges Press’s (2004: xvi) conclusion that “principled ideas offer a better explanation of motivations [for protest] than rational choice/self-interest.”Structural conditions notwithstanding, leadership – the fourth major factor – was central to the emergence and operations of the Kenyan protest movement. Effective leadership – as both agency and structure – explains how individual and collective grievances are channelled into collective action. This is because agency on the part of activists is required to recognise, utilise, and push the frontiers of existing opportunities for mobilisation (Mati 2013; McCarthy and Zald 1977; Nasong’o 2007; Katumanga 1999; Gusfield 1966; Morris and Staggenborg 2004). In the Kenyan reform struggle, it took courage, bloodshed, persecution, and organisation for this to happen. This suggests that individual as well as collective agency is an important variable in translating grievances into social movement organisations that are able to challenge collective and individual oppression.Additionally, the exercise of mass agency was aided by the mobilisation of sufficient resources by individuals with grievances. Such mobilisation occurred, as we saw in [Chapter 2](#chapter02.xhtml), because movement entrepreneurs – who embody a combination of agency and structure – catalysed and transformed collective discontent into social movements (see Tilly 1977; McAdam 1982). In the case of the Ufungamano Initiative, such entrepreneurs were critical in bringing together a wide range of pro-reform groups from civil society, religious organisations, and opposition political parties to push for constitutional reform.To highlight the importance of effective leadership, [Chapters 2](#chapter02.xhtml) and 3 demonstrated that incubating and building a movement was far from easy, especially in the context of multiple identities and competing interests and initiatives. Bringing so many diverse actors together demanded more than the mere existence of the Moi/KANU regime as a common adversary. In addition, framing, civic education, and community organising strategies combined to facilitate the emergence of shared ideals. Horse-trading among the constituent groups regarding leadership positions in the Ufungamano Initiative was critical, too. One such group was the National Council of Churches of Kenya (NCCK), whose leader agreed to bring his organisation into the movement only after he was promised a stewardship role. This notwithstanding, he subsequently became the key driver who brought the movement to life and steered it during its most impactful phase.At this point, it is important to highlight another – less positive – aspect of leadership in social movements. Specifically, fundamental contradictions in the leadership of the Ufungamano Initiative left it unable to create the structures that were needed to initiate genuine popular participation. The implication is that building socio-political struggles on individual personalities can fail spectacularly. Therefore, charismatic leadership can be both a strength and a weakness. This duality is generally ignored in the social movement literature.

## Waves of contention in Kenya

This study augments existing scholarship on the role of state structures in the emergence, operations, and achievements of socio-political protest movements in contemporary Kenya and, by extension, Africa. The methodological approach was drawn from, among others, Tilly’s (2004) proposition that the contentious nature of social movements requires a systematic socio-historical approach. This is because, as Regis Debray (1967: 19) notes, “we are never completely contemporaneous with our present. History advances in disguise; it appears on stage wearing the mask of the preceding scene.”History, therefore, offers an important lens for understanding the present. Following this approach, a historiography of social protests in Kenya in the [Chapter 1](#chapter01.xhtml) traced the key waves of contention from the anti-colonial struggles in the aftermath of the Second World War. After independence, the nationalist movement quickly disintegrated along ethnic and class lines. The result was a duality with both class and ethnic dimensions in subsequent struggles. Moreover, by the 1990s, these struggles had also acquired gender and generational dimsensions. This suggests that there were multiple secondary struggles within the main struggle, defined by class, ethnic, gender, and generational cleavages. Of these, the ethnic dimension, manipulated by elites to conceal class differences, has been the most visible. Specifically, this manifests as sporadic intra-elite conflict. That said, whenever they are threatened by a class-based revolution from below, the elites find a way to cooperate to safeguard their privileged positions. As such, both elite cleavages and elite pacts have been key features of these contentions. Among others, Press (2004) argues that the 2002 political transition in Kenya occurred in the absence of negotiations or pacts between the country’s elites. However, this ignores the fact that a section of the KANU regime left the party, signed a deal with the opposition (the infamous Memorandum of Understanding), and joined NARC. Moreover, Press fails to mention other pacts between elite formations, especially those opposed to Moi, which helped to energise the constitutional reform struggle and propel it forwards. For example, in essence, the Ufungamano Initiative itself was an elite bargain between secular civil society leaders, religious leaders, and opposition political party leaders. And the same could be said of the merger between the movement and the rival parliament-led review process, the IPPG deal of 1997, and indeed the 2010 constitution.Elite bargains were also responsible for the miscarriage of popular reforms. In this regard, they need to be understood within North *et al*.’s (2013) theory that the members of elites reinforce one another’s privileges. This is especially true in times of crisis, when their positions come under threat. In Kenya, for example, an elite pact saw the Ufungamano Initiative merge with the state-led process, resulting in subordination of the popular will. This elite bargain was possible because the religious, political, and secular civil society actors who led the movement ensured that structures for popular involvement of the lower classes were stifled, leaving all of the critical decision-making roles in their hands.This book’s historiography of social protests in contemporary Kenya argued that the first key wave of contention, which took place in the early 1990s, forced the repeal of Section 2A of the constitution, which had criminalised political opposition since 1982\. This had the effect of opening up some democratic space, but this did not necessarily translate into fair, democratic political competition. This sowed the seeds for new struggles, especially given the context of continued repression by the state.The Moi/KANU regime’s response to contestations for an open, associational space determined not only the nature of emergent social movements but also the strategies they applied in their struggles against the state (Katumanga 1999). For instance, from 1991, the Moi/KANU regime, in a desperate effort to retain power, unleashed systematic violence on opposition groups in many regions of the country (Kagwanja 2003; 2006; Wamucii and Idwasi 2011; Murunga 1999). As a result, some of the targeted ethnic groups resorted to establishing violent underground movements such as Mungiki, Kamjesh, Taliban, and the Baghdad Boys to counter state violence. This dispersal of violence capacity had some dialectic consequences. First, it allowed the state to maintain control over the reins of power and sustain itself. On the other hand, the dispersal of coercive power meant that the state became a contested space with a leadership that was incapable of quashing competition (Katumanga 1999). Due to such contestation, Wamucii and Idwasi (2011: 196) observe that some of the emergent violent underground groups “became a preferred option to [the] ineffective” state. But the emergence of these vigilante groups must also be seen as a response to the infamous ruling party militia (KANU’s youth wingers), who used violence and intimidation to maintain the regime’s hold on power (Kanyinga and Njoka 2002). In addition, far from all of the challenges to the state were violent. Indeed, most of the confrontations manifested as peaceful collective actions against state processes and activities. One such response was the Ufungamano Initiative.

## What brings disparate groups together? The story of the Ufungamano Initiative

[Chapter 3](#chapter03.xhtml) traced Kenya’s political developments from 1997 – a milestone year that ultimately led to the founding of the Ufungamano Initiative two years later. Specifically, cumulative pressures for a comprehensive constitutional review spearheaded by burgeoning human rights NGOs, religious organisations, and opposition parties climaxed in the mass protests of 1997 that threatened to derail the general election. A reluctant President Moi accepted minimal reform of Kenya’s electoral laws through the infamous IPPG agreements of 1997, negotiated between KANU and the opposition political parties. Instructively, the IPPG agreed to set up a commission to review the constitution and subsequently enacted the 1997 Constitution of Kenya Review Act, formally ushering in the state-run review process.The IPPG deal, an intra-elite pact, deflated the vibrancy of the largest constitutional reform movement at the time, the National Convention Executive Council (NCEC). This precipitated disagreements within opposition ranks, as many secular civil society activists felt betrayed by the apparent closing of ranks of the political elites, seemingly to allow them to control – if not arrest – the tide of reform. On the one hand, these divisions gave rise to new contenders in the struggle for constitutional reform. But they also prompted a shift of donor support from the NCEC to the IPPG and the resultant 1997 Constitution of Kenya Review Act. Thereafter, the state’s intransigence over implementation of the Review Act generated the necessary conditions for cooperation between secular civil society, religious organisations, and opposition political parties, which in turn facilitated the emergence of the Ufungamano Initiative. However, this cooperation was based on a mirage of the congruence of means and ends.The specific triggers for the Ufungamano Initiative are traceable to developments following the 1997 general election (see [Chapter 3](#chapter03.xhtml)). In particular, a new alliance between the Moi/KANU regime and Raila Odinga’s National Development Party (the third-largest party in the country at the time) served as a wake-up call to those opposed to Moi and KANU. There was a sense of collective outrage and a common target that enabled collective mobilisation to confront shared problems. This conforms to Oliver and Myers’s (2003) theory regarding the co-evolution of movements and their antagonists, in which each learns and adjusts in response to the other’s actions and strategies.While the Ufungamano Initiative called for fundamental changes in the architecture of the state and its relationship with the people of Kenya, this was never fully achieved because of the fragmented nature of the struggle and the dialectic nature of elite fragmentation. Together, these factors created favourable conditions for subsequent elite pacts. At this point, the critical question becomes: what exactly did the Ufungamano Initiative achieve?Scholars of African politics are quick to point to the improbability of a united movement emerging in a context of multiple identity variables. However, the Ufungamano Initiative helps us understand how disparate secular and religious civil society organisations and opposition political parties, with potentially conflicting interests, are sometimes able to join hands to confront those in power. Arguably, the Ufungamano Initiative achieved more success in the constitutional reform project than any other movement in Kenya’s recent history. It emerged with great potential and generated widespread mass support. It was also an amplification of a long history of counter-hegemonic struggles and political contentions against unjust governance, wealth creation, and resource distribution. It had several significant impacts on the constitutional reform struggle. First, the mere emergence of the Ufungamano Initiative as a unified movement was an achievement in itself. Unity had eluded many socio-political struggles in Kenya because of the prevailing politics of ethnicity coupled with deeply entrenched religious and class interests. The Ufungamano Initiative succeeded because of a congruence of interests among the leaders of religious organisations, secular civil society, and progressive intellectuals who managed to form a movement that could accommodate people with widely divergent views.Second, the Ufungamano Initiative cultivated a sense of “one nation” and faith in a better Kenya that overrode all of its inherent diversity. In this regard, its emergence challenges the prevailing social movement scholarship’s identification of identity-based consciousness (the culture, social status, ideology, general beliefs, values, ethnicity, gender, and/or sexuality of actors) as the glue that holds such movements together. Specifically, it suggests that unity can be achieved when a certain vision resonates with different social forces or speaks to a majority because of effective framing, ultimately creating a counter-hegemonic ideology. The Ufungamano Initiative further suggests that the polarisation of Kenyans is only skin deep.Third, the Ufungamano Initiative demonstrates that an autocratic state is not omnipotent when certain external and internal conditions – as well as structure and agency – converge at the right moment. The fact that the Ufungamano Initiative managed to push the government into merger negotiations without widespread violence is itself indicative of the power of a different kind of contention – direct action by citizen groups to draft a constitution that is fit for purpose. According to Cottrell and Ghai (2007: 12), such action dragged the “Moi Government into a review that it did not want.”Fourth, the Ufungamano Initiative demonstrates that active citizenship has the capacity to shape democracy. This is because, through civic education, the movement managed to demystify the constitutional review process. Hitherto, the process had been “an arcane science known by its name and understood by a few” (Lumumba 2008: 52). The Ufungamano Initiative countered this by increasing public awareness and exposing the links between the existing constitution and the material conditions in which people lived.Fifth, the Ufungamano Initiative managed to put the ordinary citizen in the driver’s seat in the push for – and realisation of – a democratic constitution-making process. This suggests that democratic processes are possible even in politically constricted environments if citizens are prepared to fight for their goals.Key to the success of the Ufungamano Initiative was the way it framed constitution making. The movement initially styled itself as a pressure group to force the state to acknowledge the people’s fundamental and sovereign right to write their own constitution. This entailed initiating a massive civic education campaign, especially among the masses, in an effort to garner their support. It was because of this cultivation of mass support that counter-hegemonic action was possible. This suggests that the Kenyan constitutional reform struggle is essentially a sphere for (counter-)hegemonic battles (Gramsci 1971). Whoever wins these battles controls the country. Viewing the struggle in this way also explains the ongoing paralysis of the state and civil society in Kenya, as there has not yet been a clear winner.Second, the movement engaged in alternative analysis and interpretation of the law. It was through such framing and analysis of the 1997 Constitution of Kenya Review Act that it was able to identify political and legal opportunities as well as the “flaws” it contested. Third, the Ufungamano Initiative capitalised on anxieties created by Moi’s refusal to accommodate other stakeholders in the constitutional reform process. Fourth, the movement framed the constitution as a sacred covenant between the people and their rulers. As such, it required inputs from both.Fifth, the Ufungamano Initiative formed the People’s Commission of Kenya (PCK) in 1999 to lead the Kenyan people in writing their own constitution when it became apparent that KANU would never allow full popular participation in the process. The PCK was the most potent of the movement’s various strategies and bodies. The launch of a parallel reform process at a time when KANU’s fidelity to the 1997 Review Act was open to question comprised the greatest challenge to the credibility and legitimacy of the state-led process. Consequently, the Moi/KANU regime was forced to seek a more independent person – Yash Pal Ghai – to lead the process. His influence was immense, especially in pushing for a merger between the state-led process and the Ufungamano Initiative.Nonetheless, the Ufungamano Initiative case also highlights the limits of social movements’ ability to challenge and reorder African states, especially after they are absorbed into the mainstream. Specifically, despite impressive early successes, the Ufungamano Initiative remained vulnerable to absorption and eventually agreed to a “coerced” merger with the state-led process in 2001\. This had the effect of dissipating the movement’s energy. What, then, can we learn from the rapid demise of such a promising protest movement?The first limitation of the Ufungamano Initiative was a product of its dependence on external funding. Regardless of their pro-democracy rhetoric, its Western donors were most concerned with protecting their own interests in what was, in effect, a neo-colony, so the movement had to temper its agenda to safeguard its funding streams. This suggests that any movement that tries to radically redefine the relationship between the people and their governors (not to mention their relationship with external capital) will fail to achieve its objectives if it remains entangled in the intricate funding webs that dictate how resources must be used. As such, although the Ufungamano Initiative – like many previous pro-reform civil society initiatives in Kenya – saw its role as rewriting the Kenyan people’s relationship with their political leaders, it was riddled with contradictions that fundamentally challenged its self-definition as a mass movement and curtailed the transformation it sought. These contradictions reflected the cleavages in wider Kenyan society. As Cottrell and Ghai (2007: 1) observe, it was because of these cleavages that, “although civil society, with much popular support, was prominent in pushing for change … vested interests … frustrated a quick outcome.”The main contradiction in the Ufungamano Initiative was the gulf between its stated objectives and its strategies. Specifically, the movement failed to establish an emancipatory structure that would ensure effective mass participation. Instead, it utilised the infrastructures of various religious institutions, which enabled it to rival the state’s structures. However, this strategy had several drawbacks. First, there was a disconnect between the rhetoric of a people-centred and people-led process and the Ufungamano Initiative’s internal practices, because the structures it utilised were led and dominated by a conservative clergy and citizens’ direct participation was not guaranteed. Second, this constrained ordinary citizens’ influence within the movement. However, this is not to suggest that greater citizen participation would have yielded different results. Indeed, as Cottrell and Ghai (2007: 12) have argued, constitution making has been highly participatory in several African countries, but the resultant constitutions have been “frustrated by governmental recalcitrance and obstruction, corruption and manipulation.”Third, despite the presence of clear lines of accountability between the different structures, a few personalities became overbearing. Ultimately, while ordinary people’s struggles were part of the Ufungamano Initiative, their token visibility betrayed the claim that the movement was a true “people’s struggle.” Such tokenism and the utilitarian use of the masses are evident in other socio-political structures in Kenya, such as political parties, some of which claim to be democratic. Therefore, the Ufungamano Initiative and the other protest movements in these contentions must be viewed within the context of an intra-elite competition for the soul of the masses. That said, the Ufungamano Initiative resonated with the public, which enabled it to mobilise considerable popular support. The question, then, is: how did the Ufungamano Initiative manage to convince the Kenyan people that it was acting in their interests? The answer lies in the fact that subaltern Kenya was awakened by the intra-elite battles for their support, especially through civic education campaigns. Moreover, this civic education enabled people to envisage an alternative future. It became a right to demonstrate and an honour to suffer in the process. In this way, ever more people identified with the cause. Additionally, ordinary citizens romanticised the Ufungamano Initiative’s ability to challenge a discredited regime.The fourth contradiction emanated from the role played by religious leaders at the helm of the Ufungamano Initiative, especially as the various Christian denominations held opposing positions on many issues. For instance, while the mainstream churches (Catholic, Methodist, Anglican, and Presbyterian) were prominent players in the struggle for change, the Evangelical Fellowship of Kenya-affiliated churches supported Moi (Karanja 2008). Muslims and the Hindus later joined the mainstream Christian groups in the Ufungamano Initiative.Initially, despite the wealth of diverse views within the Christian clergy – let alone between the Christians and other faiths – it was widely believed that the Ufungamano Initiative would benefit from a religious leadership on account of the clergy’s supposed neutrality and objectivity. However, this case demonstrates that leaders who fail to remain true and sympathetic to the popular will risk losing public confidence and trust. As soon as the Ufungamano Initiative’s leaders started fighting among themselves, introduced an ethnic dimension to their disagreements, and jostled for political power they lost much of their credibility with the masses and ultimately scuppered implementation of a new constitution in 2005\. This became increasingly apparent in the context of heightened contestation along religious and ethnic lines, which saw previously active members of the clergy betray the struggle for reform and join the pro-state camp.The failure to deliver a new constitution in 2005 did not end the dream of reform. On the contrary, new campaigns, such as *Katiba Sasa*, and political alliances emerged from the ashes to launch a new wave of contention. This climaxed in another referendum on August 4, 2010, during which Kenyans overwhelmingly voted in favour of the proposed constitution. Finally, after two decades of struggle punctuated by bitter political, ethnic, and religious divisions, Kenyans had a new constitution. These divisions were the result of a multiplicity of actors with competing interests’ attempts to capture, manage, and utilise the post-colonial state.The disintegration of the Ufungamano Initiative after the merger with the state-run review process and the public’s rejection of a new constitution in 2005 help to explain why Kenya descended into chaos in 2007/2008\. Specifically, the merger, which was based on the mirage of state acquiescence to the Ufungamano Initiative’s demands for popular democratic participation, arrested the adoption and implementation of a constitution that was fit for purpose. Moreover, it neutered the power of the Ufungamano Initiative. Another factor in the movement’s demise was the 2002 general election, which offered hope and caused the Ufungamano leaders to drop their guards and allow themselves to be co-opted into the new regime. The net effect was a flagrant betrayal of the popular will as the members of the new power elite began to consolidate their positions at the expense of reform – the banner under which they had been elected. Nyinguro and Otenyo (2007: 5) observe that the window of opportunity for reform – which “depended on elite responses to the opportunity pendulum” – closed as this new power elite focused on their own narrow interests and exploitation. In the end, then, the Ufungamano Initiative became yet another missed opportunity to transform the Kenyan state and the nature of its relations with the country’s citizens.Consequently, pent-up anger over the NARC regime’s failure to deliver a new constitution or tackle countless other associated issues (legal and institutional reform, land reform, unemployment, particularly among the youth, poverty, inequality, regional development imbalances, etc.) found expression in ethnic demagogy during the 2007 election campaign, which in turn precipitated ethnic violence following disputes over the outcome of the presidential ballot. The 2007/2008 post-election ethnic bloodbath came to an end only after the two main protagonists – Kibaki and Odinga – grudgingly agreed to a political marriage of convenience. Two years later, this elite bargain would finally deliver a new constitution.This end result suggests that a confluence of means and ends between the two main elite camps was a *sine qua non* for transforming power relations in the Kenyan polity (Mati 2013). Second, it could be said that the elite cleavages that fuelled the ethnic violence played a critical role in delivering the new constitution because the collective threat the elites faced during the 2007/2008 crisis, especially with regard to the loss of property, prompted them to negotiate bargains and make concessions among themselves. Third, the international community played a decisive role, especially in mediating the truce that facilitated the formation of a government of national unity and insisting on prompt constitutional reform. Fourth, all of these were only possible because of sustained pressure for reform from civil society. Therefore, vigilance is a necessity in the ongoing campaign for further constitutional reform, lest it becomes yet another of Kenya’s many “missed boats,” transitions without transformation, or, in the words of George Macaulay Trevelyan (1923: 292), turning points when history fails to turn (Muluka 2011; Currie and Ray 1986).It is already evident that the 2010 constitution was something of a false dawn, because it never really cured the crises that have informed the constitutional reform struggle over the past three decades. It transformed certain state institutions and practices, and devolution has created some opportunities for the masses to alter their socioeconomic reality. However, a greater effect of devolution has been simply to widen the political elite by dispersing rent-seeking opportunities. Moreover, Kenya is still a long way from achieving a just and truly democratic political order; ethnic identity continues to be the master frame in most political mobilisations; and predatory elite behaviour is far from contained. Under these circumstances, a new wave of contention was evident in the protests before and after the 2017 presidential election. This wave’s progress was temporarily halted by the elite bargain – and symbolic handshake – between President Uhuru Kenyatta and his main competitor, Raila Odinga, on March 9, 2018\. However, their truce also had the effect of fracturing Kalenjin–Kikuyu unity. At the time of writing, the jury was still out on the long-term implications of these developments.Note1    Kibwana, author interview, October 21, 2009.
